Anthropic launches IPO roadshow with $64B fundraising goal

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Anthropic PBC today kicked off the roadshow for its upcoming initial public offering.

An IPO roadshow is a series of promotional events aimed at persuading institutional investors to buy shares. Anthropic plans to sell 825 million units of common stock for $70 to $78 per share, which works out to $64.35 billion at the top end of the range. About 23 million of the shares will be offered by the company’s seven co-founders, who are each set to make $250 million.

The funding haul could increase significantly if the banks underwriting the IPO exercise their over-allotment option. Anthropic will enable them to buy nearly 124 million shares within 30 days of the IPO. At the top end of the company’s target pricing, that stock would be worth $965 million.

Anthropic logged $17 billion in revenue and a $536 million operating loss last quarter. Its pitch to roadshow participants will likely emphasize its rapid growth. According to the company’s IPO filing, it generated about seven times more revenue during the first nine months of the year than in all of 2025.

Anthropic’s strategy for maintaining its sales growth doesn’t rely solely on new model releases. It’s also building out a portfolio of tools that make its models easier to use. The three newest additions to the lineup made their debut today alongside the launch of the IPO roadshow.

The first offering, Claude Meetings, is an artificial intelligence assistant designed for video conferences. Workers can use it to retrieve information from business applications and generate project plans. They can then use Claude Whiteboard, the second tool that Anthropic debuted today, to brainstorm the AI-generated content and make revisions.

The company’s third new product is also designed for productivity tasks. Claude Sheets is a spreadsheet editor with a natural language interface. It can be used together with Claude Data, an existing Anthropic offering previously known as Claude Dashboards that moved into general availability today. It can automatically turn Claude-generated spreadsheets and other files into dashboards.

Goldman Sachs, one of the IPO’s lead underwriters, reportedly estimates that Anthropic will end the year with about $52 billion in revenue. According to the Financial Times, the bank expects that number to grow more than tenfold by 2030.

Goldman Sachs reportedly believes Anthropic will achieve positive free cash flow in the same time frame. Free cash flow is a profitability metric that measures a company’s revenue minus operating expenses.

In conjunction with Anthropic’s IPO, seven of its top suppliers will buy $26 billion worth of shares through a private placement. The group includes Nvidia Corp., Advanced Micro Devices Inc., Broadcom Inc., Google LLC, Micron Technology Inc., Microsoft Corp. and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. Reuters reported that TSMC also plans to invest $500 million in OpenAI Group PBC’s IPO.

Anthropic is expected to price its shares on Nov. 18. The company will list on the Nasdaq under the ticker symbol “ANT.”

Germany U21 squad update | Three players earn late call-ups following injury withdrawals

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Germany U21 head coach Antonio Di Salvo has been forced to make three changes to his original squad for November’s internationals against Belgium and Croatia following the injury-related withdrawals of Lukas Ullrich, Mika Baur, and Arijon Ibrahimovic.

Borussia Mönchengladbach defender Ullrich suffered a broken collarbone during Saturday’s defeat to Hamburger SV, while Celtic midfielder Baur has been ruled out with an ankle injury. The nature of Ibrahimovic’s injury has not been disclosed.

As confirmed by the DFB, Union Berlin defender Tom Rothe, Hamburger SV midfielder Kofi Amoako, and SV Elversberg’s Maurice Krattenmacher have been called up as replacements. Amoako and Krattenmacher are in line for their U21 debuts, while Rothe returns to the national-team setup.

Meanwhile, Racing Strasbourg defender Karim Coulibaly remains doubtful after experiencing illness symptoms. Di Salvo indicated on Monday that a decision on his participation would be made following further assessment.

Germany face Belgium in Essen on Friday, November 13, before travelling to Rijeka to face Croatia four days later. Both fixtures form part of Germany’s preparations for next summer’s U21 European Championship in Albania and Serbia.

Delta SkyMiles Gold Card Review: Solid Perks For Occasional Delta Flyers

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Delta SkyMiles® Gold American Express Card is the mid-range personal credit card in the American Express and Delta co-brand card portfolio, which includes seven cards, covering a variety of annual fee levels, across both personal and business products.

This post is my in-depth review of the Delta SkyMiles Gold Amex Card. The card has a reasonable annual fee and is ideal for someone looking for basic benefits that come in handy when flying Delta. However, if you’re a Medallion elite flyer on Delta, another card might be a better option (more on that below).

Link: Learn more about the Delta SkyMiles® Gold American Express Card

Before I get into my Delta Gold SkyMiles American Express Card review, here’s a quick look at the card’s main selling points and fees:

Here are my thoughts on the pros and cons of the Delta Gold SkyMiles Credit Card:

To start, let’s take a look at the Delta Gold Amex welcome bonus and ongoing fees, since these are some of the most important features to consider when opening a new card.

The Delta Gold SkyMiles Amex is currently offering new cardholders the following bonus: You may be eligible for as high as 80,000 Bonus Miles after you spend $2,000 in eligible purchases on your new Card in your first 6 months of Card Membership. Welcome offers vary and you may not be eligible for an offer.

Based on my latest miles & points valuations, SkyMiles are worth 1.1 cents each. That means to me, the welcome bonus is worth up to $880.

This is a solid welcome bonus, though the card recently offered a higher one that included a statement credit along with bonus miles. Everyone has to decide for themselves whether they’d rather wait for a better offer, or apply with this offer so they can take advantage of the perks ASAP.

The welcome offer on the Delta Gold Amex card is “once in a lifetime,” so it’s not available to you if you currently have the card, or if you’ve had the card in the past.

You’re also not eligible for the welcome offer on this card if you have or have had the personal versions of the Delta SkyMiles® Platinum American Express Card (learn more) or the Delta SkyMiles® Reserve American Express Card (learn more). However, you are eligible for this card if you’ve had any version of the business Delta Amex cards before.

One thing worth emphasizing is that if you’re going to apply for multiple personal Delta Amex cards, you’ll want to pick up the Delta Gold Amex first. That’s because if you do, you’d potentially still be eligible for the Delta Platinum Amex and Delta Reserve Amex. Meanwhile if you apply for one of those cards first, you’d no longer be eligible for the “lesser” card.

When it comes to credit scores, there’s not a consistent score you need to get approved for the Delta Gold Amex. I’d hope to have a score in the “good” to “excellent” range, ideally 700+ points. However, people with lower scores have been approved, and people with higher scores have been denied, as it’s only one of many factors that determines card eligibility.

You can read more about Amex’s general application restrictions here.

The Delta Gold SkyMiles American Express Card has a $0 introductory annual fee for the first year, then $150. (Rates & Fees). This is a great opportunity to try the card before you have to “buy” it. The card also has a reasonable annual fee on an ongoing basis, so it’s easy to recoup that fee by taking advantage of the card perks.

You can also add authorized users to the Delta Gold Amex at no extra cost.

If you are going to put spending on the card, the good news is that there are no foreign transaction fees (Rates & Fees) with the Delta Gold Amex, so you can use the card abroad without paying those high fees.

When applying for a credit card, the rewards structure is obviously a major consideration for people. With airline credit cards, the question typically comes down to both the miles offered on spending, as well as the ability to qualify for elite status with spending. Unfortunately the Delta Gold Amex isn’t much to get excited about on that front, but let’s still go over the details.

When it comes to the return on spending, the Delta Gold Card offers:

While it’s nice that the card has some bonus categories, I don’t find the bonuses to be particularly compelling. There are still better cards out there for airfare purchases, restaurant purchases, and grocery store purchases.

Nowadays it’s common to see airlines offer elite status qualification through credit card spending. Delta SkyMiles elite status is based on the concept of Medallion Qualifying Dollars (MQDs), and it’s possible to earn elite status with credit card spending.

The catch is that the Delta Gold Card doesn’t earn any MQDs, so if you want to qualify for status, you’ll have to pick up the Delta Platinum Card or Delta Reserve Card. More on that later.

While Delta SkyMiles is far from my favorite mileage currency, it does have a high floor value, so there are good ways to redeem Delta SkyMiles. You can redeem SkyMiles for flights on Delta or on Delta’s SkyTeam partners, like Air France, China Airlines, and Korean Air.

Partner award flights can be an especially good option if you focus on flights that don’t touch the United States, since flights that do are more expensive and get you less value for your miles. I’ve also seen some good award prices for Delta One (business class) awards, specifically for booking roundtrip rather than one way.

As I’ll cover below, having the Delta Gold Amex also gives you access to the TakeOff 15 discount on award tickets, and that can also help you stretch your SkyMiles for travel on Delta.

The real reason you’ll want to keep the Delta Gold Card long term is because of the perks and benefits that it offers, which are great for the casual Delta flyer. Truthfully, this is the value proposition of many airline credit cards — it’s largely about the welcome bonus and the perks, so let’s take a closer look at those details for the Delta Gold Amex.

Perhaps one of the biggest incentives to actually spend money on the Delta Gold SkyMiles Credit Card is the ability to earn a flight credit. Specifically, if you spend $10,000 on the card in a calendar year, you can receive a $200 Delta eCredit to use toward future travel, which will be deposited right in your SkyMiles account. The credit expires one year after it’s issued.

Getting a $200 Delta eCredit in addition to any miles could be a good incentive to spend $10,000 on the card per year. The credit alone is the equivalent of a 2% return on spending, assuming you consider it to be worth face value.

Just for having the Delta Gold Card you receive up to $100 in Delta Stays credit annually. The way this works, you receive up to $100 in statement credits per calendar year when you use your card to pay for a new Delta Stays prepaid hotel or vacation rental booking on the Delta Stays platform.

While I’m not usually a fan of using airline portals to book hotel stays and vacation rentals, this could prove worthwhile, as you can use it for a hotel stay costing right around $100, if you wanted to. If used right, the value here is considerable.

Just for having the Delta Gold SkyMiles Amex, you get a first checked bag free. You can also receive a second checked bag free on eligible domestic Delta flights.

This benefit is limited to the primary card member and up to eight companions traveling on the same reservation. You don’t have to pay for your flight with your Delta Amex, though you do need to have your SkyMiles number in the reservation.

With the ever-increasing cost of a checked bag with Delta, this can really add up. A first checked bag on domestic flights costs $45, and a second costs $55, so even just checking one bag on two roundtrip flights could effectively cancel out the card’s annual fee.

You receive priority boarding just for having the Delta Gold Amex. This helps maximize your odds of finding space for your carry-on and getting to your seat sooner. To receive this benefit, you must ensure the primary card member’s SkyMiles number is linked to the reservation. This benefit is valid for the primary card member and up to eight companions traveling on the same reservation.

If you have the Delta Gold Amex then you can save 20% in the form of a statement credit for eligible inflight purchases of food and beverages. The statement credit will post within 8-12 weeks of an eligible purchase.

Delta has a Pay With Miles feature, where if you have a Delta co-branded credit card, you can redeem miles for 1.0 cent each toward a Delta flight purchase (in increments of 5,000 miles per $50). That’s not the best deal in the world, though this is a feature that’s exclusively available to those with an eligible card.

Some people find this to be useful since they can redeem their miles toward flights, and then they still earn miles and MQDs on the portion of the fare they pay in cash.

With the Delta TakeOff 15 perk (exclusively for those with select Delta Amex cards), you can save 15% on the cost of a SkyMiles award ticket when traveling exclusively on Delta flights booked through delta.com or the Fly Delta app. Delta SkyMiles award pricing is dynamic, and generally you’ll find that each mile gets you a bit over a cent toward the cost of a ticket.

Saving 15% on these awards is valuable, and means that you’ll generally get somewhere around 1.2-1.3 cents of value per mile (or sometimes even more). In many cases this will end up being a better value than using the Pay With Miles option, so it’s a real perk of the card.

The Delta Gold SkyMiles Credit Card also comes with a rideshare credit. The way it works is that you get up to $10 back as a statement credits each month when you use the card on U.S. rideshare purchases with select providers, including Uber and Lyft.

You need to enroll in this benefit in order to get the money back, and it doesn’t kick in until after your first card renewal. So while it’s a useful perk if you already pay for rideshares at least once a month, it doesn’t really make or break the card.

One of the great features of Amex cards is access to Amex Offers, which offers savings or bonus points on purchases with all kinds of retailers. There’s huge value to be had in having as many Amex cards as possible, so that you can get these offers on multiple cards.

Amex Offers could save you hundreds of dollars per year. You can access these offers by logging into your account and scrolling down to the bottom of your account summary page. Note that enrollment is required to take advantage of individual offers.

There are some coverage limits and exclusions to be aware of, so you’ll want to review the full details in the card’s benefits guide before assuming you’re insured.

Lincoln Recalls 37K Navigators for Front Seat That May Move on Its Own

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The recall affects 2025–2027 models with the ‘Chauffeur Seat Feature.’ Greasy fingers may play a role.

No one likes greasy fingers, especially when they might cause a power-adjustable seat to move on its own. That’s the issue plaguing nearly 37,000 Lincoln Navigators, which are being recalled because their front passenger seat may unexpectedly move.

The recall involves 2025–2027 Navigator models with the “Chauffeur Seat Feature.” This function allows people sitting in the second row to control the front passenger seat’s movements via a touchscreen that’s built into the center console.

Based on the recall documents that Lincoln submitted to the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA), residue on the touch-activated display may cause the front passenger seat to unexpectedly move forward or back or recline up or down. Plus, if the home screen button is pressed on the rear touchscreen at the same time as a seat adjustment, Lincoln says this may cause the seat to move all the way to the end of its track without any buttons being pressed again.

See all results for new 2026 Lincoln Navigator for sale near 94112

While an empty seat that moves on its own is mostly just spooky, an occupied one could potentially cause an injury. That’s why Lincoln is asking owners to bring their affected Navigators to an authorized dealer so that the Chauffeur Seat Feature can be deactivated. This will be done for free, but it doesn’t sound like that function will work anymore. Finally, Lincoln says that both dealers and owners should have been notified by mail by now. If you haven’t received a letter and want to know if your SUV is part of the recall, you can use this feature on the NHTSA website to look up your VIN or search by year, make, and model, which will also show any other recall-related alerts.

From House charges to a Senate verdict: 10 parts of U.S. impeachment

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Learn how impeachment works in the U.S., from House charges and articles of impeachment to the Senate trial, the two-thirds conviction vote and what removal means

Impeachment is an accusation, not an eviction. When the House of Representatives impeaches a president or a federal judge, that official stays in office. Removal can come only later, and only if the Senate votes to convict. That distinction is the one most often blurred in headlines, cable segments and social media posts whenever the word starts trending.

The U.S. Constitution splits the job between the two chambers of Congress by design. The House acts something like a grand jury, deciding whether the evidence justifies formal charges. The Senate acts as the court, hearing the case and delivering a verdict. Each chamber has its own vote threshold, its own procedures and its own political pressures. An official can be impeached by the first and still be acquitted by the second.

The process is rare, but it is not theoretical. The House has impeached presidents four times, and three different men have faced a Senate trial as a result. None was removed. Federal judges are a different story: they are the only officials the Senate has ever convicted. Another president left office before the full House could vote at all.

The Constitution sketches the process in a few short clauses. Much of the detail has been filled in by practice. The House writes its own rules for investigating. The Senate sets its own procedures for trying cases. The Constitution never defines the key phrase describing impeachable conduct, and Congress has interpreted it case by case.

Understanding how impeachment works makes those open questions easier to follow. The 10 slides ahead walk through the process in order. They start with what the word means under the Constitution and who can be impeached. They then cover the grounds for charges, how the House opens an inquiry and how it votes on articles. Next come the Senate trial, the two-thirds bar for conviction and the penalties that follow a conviction. The final slides look back at the presidents who have been impeached. They also compare impeachment with two other routes by which a president can lose power: the 25th Amendment and resignation.

Each slide stands on its own. A reader can go straight to the stage of the process that matters most to them.

Impeachment is the formal act of charging a federal official with misconduct. It is not the act of removing that person from office. Under the U.S. Constitution, the House of Representatives brings the charges, and the Senate decides whether they hold up at trial.

News coverage and everyday conversation often use the word loosely, as if it meant being thrown out of office. That reading skips a step. An official who has been impeached has been accused, much as a criminal defendant who has been indicted stands accused. Neither has been found guilty of anything yet.

The comparison to a criminal case holds only in broad outline. Impeachment is a constitutional proceeding run by Congress, not a prosecution in a courtroom. The two chambers set many of their own procedures for it.

The House's authority comes from Article I, Section 2 of the Constitution. That clause gives the House "the sole Power of Impeachment." No other body can bring the charges.

The Senate's role comes from Article I, Section 3. That section gives the Senate "the sole Power to try all Impeachments." Only the Senate can hold the trial, and only the Senate can deliver the verdict.

Both clauses sit in Article I, the part of the Constitution that establishes Congress. That placement puts impeachment in the hands of the legislature. Courts and the executive branch do not bring the charges or decide the outcome.

The split between the two chambers has a practical consequence. An impeachment by the House, on its own, does not change whether the official holds the job. The person stays in office while the Senate considers the case. If the Senate acquits, the official keeps the position. The impeachment itself still remains part of the official record.

This two-stage design is the core of the process. Every later step — the inquiry, the vote on charges, the trial and any penalty — follows from the division of labor written into those two sections of Article I.

The Constitution allows Congress to impeach the president, the vice president and civil officers of the U.S. government. That coverage, set out in Article II, Section 4, also reaches federal judges. Members of Congress, by contrast, are generally not treated as civil officers who can be impeached.

The presidency draws the most attention, but it is one office among several on the list. Article II, Section 4 places the vice president in the same clause as the president. The two highest executive offices therefore face an identical process. Beyond them, the clause extends to the wider category of civil officers serving in the federal government.

Federal judges hold a distinct place on that list. Their position differs from that of elected officials in one basic way. Article III says federal judges hold office during good behavior. That language gives them no fixed term and no reelection campaign to face. Impeachment gives Congress a constitutional route to end that tenure.

The status of lawmakers was tested early in the country's history. In 1797, the House impeached Senator William Blount. The Senate dismissed the case in 1799 on jurisdictional grounds. A dismissal on those grounds turns on whether the Senate had authority over the case, not on whether the accusations were true.

Rank offers no protection under Article II, Section 4. The clause covers the most senior official in the executive branch, and nothing in its text shields any covered officeholder because of seniority. What the clause does limit is the basis for an impeachment. It ties the process to stated grounds, not to the importance of the person accused.

That structure gives the clause two separate filters. The first asks whether a person holds one of the covered offices. The second asks whether the conduct at issue fits the grounds the Constitution names. Both questions apply to every case brought under the clause.

The Constitution allows impeachment on three grounds: treason, bribery and "other high crimes and misdemeanors." It never defines that last phrase. Congress has given it meaning one case at a time.

The list appears in Article II, Section 4. That clause names the officials who can face impeachment and, in the same sentence, the conduct that can lead to it.

Treason and bribery are named outright. The third category works differently. No clause anywhere in the constitutional text supplies a definition, and no list of qualifying offenses accompanies it. A reader looking for a checklist of impeachable conduct will not find one in the document.

That gap leaves interpretation to Congress. When the House weighs articles of impeachment, its members decide whether the conduct described fits the phrase. When the Senate holds a trial, senators reach their own judgment on the same question. Each vote reflects how individual members read the phrase. The same conduct can draw different answers from different lawmakers.

One question has stayed open: whether an impeachable act must also be an indictable crime. An indictable crime is one that could be charged in a criminal court. Scholars and lawmakers have disagreed on this point. One reading limits the phrase to conduct that breaks a criminal law. Another treats it as broad enough to reach conduct that is not a crime. The Constitution does not resolve the dispute.

The two readings lead to different kinds of charges. Under the narrower reading, an article of impeachment would need to describe conduct that a prosecutor could also take to a criminal court. Under the broader reading, an article could rest on conduct such as a misuse of an office's powers, even where no criminal statute covers it.

The disagreement shapes how impeachment debates unfold. A defense that a charge describes no crime is an argument about this phrase. So is a claim that an official's conduct warrants removal whether or not it breaks a criminal law. Both depend on how members of Congress read a single phrase in Article II, Section 4.

An impeachment inquiry begins in the House of Representatives, where any member can introduce a resolution calling for an official to be impeached. The House Judiciary Committee has usually taken charge of the investigation that follows, though the Constitution sets no fixed procedure for this stage.

Filing a resolution is only a starting point. It does not charge anyone, and it does not commit the House to act. A single lawmaker can put one forward, but the chamber decides whether anything comes of it.

The committee's task at this stage is to establish what happened. It holds hearings and gathers evidence, building the factual record that any later charges would rest on.

The full House can also step in directly. Members can vote to authorize an inquiry, putting the chamber's formal backing behind the investigation.

The Constitution is silent on how any of this should be done. It gives the House the power to impeach but says nothing about who investigates, how evidence is collected or what steps must come before a vote. The House fills that gap itself by setting its own rules.

That arrangement means the inquiry stage is shaped by the chamber's choices rather than by a constitutional script. One inquiry can begin with a member's resolution and committee hearings. Another can begin with a floor vote authorizing the investigation. Both paths fit within the House's authority.

The inquiry is also distinct from what comes later. It is a fact-finding phase, not a trial, and it produces no verdict. No official is impeached during an inquiry. Impeachment itself happens only when the full House approves formal charges.

For readers following an impeachment in the news, the word "inquiry" marks this early, investigative step. It signals that the House is examining an official's conduct. It does not mean the House has decided to bring charges, and it does not mean the official faces removal.

The House impeaches an official by approving at least one article of impeachment, the formal written charge against that person. Each article needs a simple majority of House members voting. If all 435 members cast a vote, 218 votes are enough to pass an article.

Each article sets out one specific allegation of misconduct against the named official. A single impeachment resolution can carry one article or several.

The House does not vote on the package as a whole. Members vote on each article on its own. That allows a lawmaker to support one charge and oppose another. It also means a resolution can leave the House with some articles approved and others rejected.

The majority standard is tied to members voting, not to the full size of the chamber. The 218 figure applies only when all 435 seats are filled and every member votes. A vacant seat, after a death or resignation, cannot cast a vote. When fewer members take part, the number needed for a majority falls with them. If 410 members vote, for example, 206 votes would pass an article. A tie vote fails. A majority means more than half of the votes cast.

Approval of one article is enough. Once an article passes, the official has been impeached, regardless of what the Senate later decides.

After the vote, the House names managers to carry the case forward. The managers are themselves members of the House. They act as prosecutors once the matter moves to the Senate. They present the charges contained in the approved articles and argue for conviction.

Only the articles that win a majority travel to the Senate. The House delivers the approved articles to the Senate, which then organizes the trial. A charge the House rejects is not part of the impeachment. The Senate does not weigh it at trial.

A Senate impeachment trial turns senators into jurors who weigh the House's charges and decide whether to convict. House managers argue the case, the official's defense team answers it, and the Senate votes on each article separately.

That jury role begins with an oath or affirmation. Every senator takes one before hearing the case. Outside a trial, senators debate and pass legislation. During one, they sit in judgment of a specific official on specific charges. The affirmation exists as an alternative for senators who prefer not to swear an oath.

Who presides depends on who is on trial. When the president is the defendant, Article I, Section 3 of the Constitution requires the chief justice of the Supreme Court to preside. That requirement applies only to presidential trials. It places the head of the federal judiciary in the chair.

The prosecution comes from the House. Its case is presented by House members known as impeachment managers. In the Senate chamber, those managers lay out the evidence and arguments behind each charge. The accused official is represented by a defense team, which responds to the managers and argues for acquittal. Senators hear both sides before any vote is taken.

The verdict is not a single up-or-down vote on the whole case. Senators vote on each article on its own. The result can differ from one charge to the next. A conviction on any single article is enough to convict the official, even if the other articles fail. Each conviction vote requires two-thirds of the senators present.

Federal courts have left these procedures to the Senate. In Nixon v. United States, decided in 1993, the Supreme Court held that challenges to the Senate's trial procedures were nonjusticiable. In practical terms, that means courts will not hear such disputes. The Senate decides for itself how to run an impeachment trial. The case involved Judge Walter Nixon, not President Richard Nixon.

Convicting an impeached official takes the votes of two-thirds of the senators present, a requirement written into Article I, Section 3 of the Constitution. When all 100 senators vote, at least 67 must vote to convict.

The threshold is measured against attendance, not against the full size of the chamber. An absent senator does not count toward the base used to calculate two-thirds. If 99 senators are present, conviction takes 66 votes. If 98 are present, 65 votes would fall just short of two-thirds, so conviction still takes 66.

The same math shows how few votes it takes to stop a conviction. With every senator voting, 34 votes against an article are enough to defeat it. The blocking number stays at 34 when 99 senators are present, because 66 votes would still be needed to convict. That means a bloc of just over one-third of the chamber can end the case on that charge.

The Senate standard sits well above the simple majority the House uses to impeach. The same two-thirds standard applies whether the accused official is a president, a federal judge or another civil officer.

The higher Senate number has a political consequence. Conviction generally requires votes from senators of both parties. For one party to convict on its own, it would need at least 67 seats, with all 100 senators present and every member of that party voting together.

Seat counts show how much cross-party support a conviction needs. A party holding 50 seats, with all 100 senators present, would need at least 17 votes from outside its ranks. A party holding 60 seats would still need seven. In practice, the side pressing for conviction has to persuade members of the opposing party.

That threshold has never been reached in a presidential case. No president has been convicted by the Senate.

If no article reaches two-thirds, the official is acquitted on every charge and keeps the job.

A Senate conviction removes the official from office. The Senate can also bar that person from holding federal office in the future. The Constitution allows no other penalty in an impeachment case.

Article I, Section 3 limits judgment in cases of impeachment to removal from office and disqualification from holding federal office. The Senate cannot impose a fine, order prison time or seize property as part of an impeachment judgment. An impeachment trial decides whether someone may hold federal office. It does not decide whether that person is punished as a criminal.

Removal comes with the conviction itself. Once the Senate convicts on an article, the official loses the office.

Disqualification works differently. It is optional, and the Senate has decided it through a separate vote. That vote has required only a simple majority. The case of Judge Thomas Porteous shows how the two steps fit together. The Senate removed Porteous from the federal bench in 2010. Then, in its own vote, it barred him from holding future federal office.

The two penalties do different things. Removal ends the official's time in their current post. Disqualification looks forward and closes off future federal office.

Leaving office through impeachment does not end the possibility of criminal charges. Article I, Section 3 states that the convicted party remains "liable and subject to Indictment, Trial, Judgment and Punishment, according to Law." Prosecutors may bring an ordinary criminal case over the same conduct. Such a case would run through the regular courts under criminal law, not under Senate trial rules.

The president's pardon power has a specific limit here. Article II, Section 2 lets the president grant reprieves and pardons for offenses against the U.S., "except in Cases of Impeachment." A president cannot use a pardon to undo an impeachment or reverse a Senate conviction. No pardon can restore a removed official to office or lift a bar on future federal service.

Three U.S. presidents have been impeached by the House of Representatives: Andrew Johnson, Bill Clinton and Donald Trump. The Senate acquitted all three men every time, and none was removed from office.

Andrew Johnson was the first. The House impeached him in 1868, which sent the case to the Senate for trial. Senators acquitted him by a single vote. One more vote to convict would have removed him from office.

Johnson's case stood alone for 130 years. The House did not impeach another president until 1998.

That president was Bill Clinton. The House impeached him on December 19, 1998. His Senate trial ran into the next year. Senators acquitted Clinton on February 12, 1999, less than two months after the House vote. He stayed in office. He remains the only president impeached in the 20th century.

Donald Trump became the third impeached president about 21 years after Clinton. The House first impeached him in December 2019. The Senate acquitted him on February 5, 2020.

The House impeached Trump again on January 13, 2021, roughly 13 months after the first time. The Senate acquitted him one month later, on February 13, 2021. Trump is the only president to face two impeachments. They came in consecutive years, the shortest gap between any two presidential impeachments.

The second trial centered on a single article of impeachment. Senators voted 57 to 43. A majority of the chamber sided with conviction. The tally still fell short of the two-thirds threshold the Constitution sets for a guilty verdict.

Richard Nixon is absent from this list because the full House never voted to impeach him.

Federal judges have a different record. They are the only federal officials the Senate has convicted. The House has impeached 15 federal judges, and eight of them were convicted. The other seven ended without a conviction. No presidential impeachment has ended that way.

Impeachment charges an official with misconduct. The 25th Amendment addresses a president who cannot do the job, and resignation is a voluntary exit. The three are often mentioned together, but they work in different ways and serve different purposes.

The 25th Amendment was ratified in 1967. It governs presidential disability and succession. Its focus is whether the president is able to serve, not how the president has behaved in office.

Section 4 of the amendment allows the vice president and a majority of the cabinet to declare that the president is unable to serve. That decision starts inside the executive branch. Impeachment, by contrast, begins with charges brought by the House of Representatives.

The 25th Amendment is not a punishment process. It does not accuse the president of wrongdoing. It involves no articles, no prosecutors and no trial. An impeachment, on the other hand, is a formal accusation that an official has committed an offense serious enough to justify removal. The 25th Amendment applies only to the president, while impeachment reaches the vice president, federal judges and other civil officers as well.

Resignation works differently from both. When an official steps down, the impeachment process usually ends without a Senate verdict. No senator votes to convict or acquit. The question of guilt is never formally resolved.

Richard Nixon followed this path. He resigned on August 9, 1974. By then, the House Judiciary Committee had approved articles of impeachment against him. The full House never held a floor vote on those articles. Nixon was never impeached by the full House.

Resignation does not always stop Congress, though. In 1876, the House impeached William Belknap, the former secretary of war, despite his resignation. The case went to a Senate trial, and the Senate acquitted him.

Of the three routes, impeachment is the only one that runs through both chambers of Congress. It is also the only one that ends in a vote on specific charges.

What executive orders can and can't do: 10 powers and limits explained

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

A guide to what executive orders can and can't do, covering 10 points on presidential authority, emergency powers, court challenges, congressional limits and revocation by later presidents

An executive order carries the weight of the presidency, yet none of its power is its own. The authority behind it comes from the Constitution or from a law Congress has already passed. That borrowed footing explains both how much an order can accomplish and where it stops.

Executive orders shape daily life in ways that are easy to miss. They tell federal agencies which laws to enforce first and how to read ambiguous statutes. They set terms for the civil servants who run the government and for the companies that sell it goods and services. A single signature can change how a regulation is drafted or how a federal contract is written.

That reach draws heavy news coverage. It also leaves many readers with a reasonable question: are executive orders law? The short answer is that they carry legal force inside the executive branch, but they are not statutes. Congress writes statutes. The president directs the people and agencies that carry them out.

Understanding what executive orders can and can't do starts with three checks built into the U.S. system. The Constitution sets the outer boundary. An order that violates it is invalid, no matter how sweeping its language. Congress holds the power to make laws and to fund the government. That means it can narrow an order's reach or override it outright. The federal courts decide disputes over whether a president stayed within those limits. The Supreme Court has struck down presidential action that went beyond them.

The fourth limit is the next president. An order does not expire when the president who signed it leaves office, unless its own text says so. But the next president can revoke or rewrite it with a new order, without any vote in Congress.

The 10 slides that follow cover a selection of these powers and limits. They begin with where an order's authority comes from, then turn to agency enforcement, federal workforce and contractor rules and national emergency declarations. Later slides cover lawmaking and spending, constitutional rights, judicial review and congressional action. The last two address states and private citizens, then revocation by a later president. Each slide stands on its own.

Every executive order must rest on one of two foundations. One is a power the Constitution gives the president in Article II. The other is a power Congress has delegated to the executive branch through a statute. An order that rests on neither cannot be enforced.

An executive order is a written directive from the president to the federal government. It tells officials and agencies what to do. It is not a law passed by Congress, and its strength depends entirely on the source it cites.

The Constitution never uses the phrase "executive order." The practice grew out of the broad grant of power in Article II. That article opens by vesting "the executive Power" in a president. It also makes the president commander in chief of the armed forces. And it directs the president to "take Care that the Laws be faithfully executed."

Those clauses give the president room to manage the executive branch. They do not give unlimited room. An order grounded in Article II has to fit within powers the Constitution actually assigns to the office.

The second foundation is delegation. Congress often writes statutes that authorize the president to act in a specific area. When an order relies on such a law, its reach depends on the statute's wording. If the law grants narrow authority, the order is narrow too.

Many orders state their legal basis in the opening lines. A typical order cites the Constitution and the laws of the U.S. as the source of the president's authority. That citation tells agencies and courts where the power to act comes from.

Executive orders are numbered in sequence and published in the Federal Register, the federal government's daily journal of official documents. The Federal Register Act of 1935 requires publication of presidential orders that have general applicability and legal effect. Publication creates a permanent public record that anyone can read.

The numbering system arrived long after the practice began. The State Department started assigning numbers in 1907. It worked backward to cover earlier orders, designating an 1862 order from President Abraham Lincoln as Executive Order 1.

A president can use an executive order to tell federal agencies how to carry out laws Congress has already passed. That includes which cases to prioritize, how to read an unclear provision and how firmly to enforce a rule.

This power follows from the president's position at the head of the executive branch. Cabinet departments and most federal agencies answer to the White House. An order is the formal, written way a president gives them instructions.

The main limit is that the order works inside existing law. It shapes how an agency uses authority it already has. It does not hand the agency new authority that Congress never granted.

Enforcement priorities are a common use. A statute may cover far more conduct than an agency has staff or budget to police. An order can tell the agency where to focus first and which cases to set aside.

Interpretation is a second lever. Laws often leave gaps or use broad terms. An order can steer agencies toward one reading of that language. That reading then guides their regulations and decisions. Courts can still review whether an agency's reading fits the statute.

Executive Order 9981 shows how far this kind of direction can reach. President Harry Truman signed it on July 26, 1948. It declared a policy of equality of treatment and opportunity for everyone in the armed services, without regard to race, color, religion or national origin.

Truman did not need Congress to pass a new law first. He acted under his authority as commander in chief, which Article II of the Constitution gives the president. That authority covers the military. An order to the armed forces fell squarely within it.

The order also created a presidential committee to recommend how the services should put the new policy into practice. It called for changes as rapidly as possible without impairing efficiency or morale. Full desegregation of the services took years after the signing.

The example illustrates a broader pattern. An order carries the most weight when it addresses officials the president already supervises, using powers the president already holds.

A president can use executive orders to set workplace rules for the federal workforce and for companies that do business with the government. The president runs the executive branch. That role includes oversight of how the government hires its workers and how it buys goods and services.

Executive Order 11246, signed by President Lyndon Johnson in 1965, set nondiscrimination requirements for federal contractors. Companies that wanted government business had to accept those terms as a condition of their contracts.

The order did not cover every employer in the U.S. Its reach came through the contracting relationship. A business with no federal contract fell outside its terms. That is the basic logic of contractor orders. The government sets conditions on the deals it signs.

Johnson's order was amended in 1967 by Executive Order 11375, which added sex discrimination to the covered categories. The change came through a new order, not through an act of Congress.

Contractor orders often rest on the Federal Property and Administrative Services Act of 1949, commonly called the Procurement Act. That law gives the president authority to set procurement policy for the executive branch. Courts have treated it as a basis for many contractor requirements.

That authority has limits. In 1995, President Bill Clinton issued Executive Order 12954, which barred certain contractors from permanently replacing striking workers. In Chamber of Commerce v. Reich, decided in 1996, a federal appeals court held that the National Labor Relations Act preempted the order.

The president has more direct control over the federal workforce itself. A federal statute, 5 U.S.C. 7301, lets the president prescribe rules of conduct for executive branch employees. In 1962, President John F. Kennedy issued Executive Order 10988, which allowed federal employees to bargain collectively through unions.

Orders like these can change hiring practices, workplace standards and contract clauses without new legislation. They still operate inside the civil service and procurement laws Congress has written. When an order conflicts with one of those statutes, the statute controls.

A national emergency declaration does not give the president new power on its own. Under the National Emergencies Act of 1976, a declaration activates authorities that other federal statutes already tie to an emergency.

The declaration and the power are two different things. The act lets the president declare a national emergency, but that step works like a key rather than a source of authority. What the president can do afterward depends on the separate laws Congress wrote to take effect during an emergency.

The act also sets procedural requirements. A president must publish the declaration in the Federal Register and send it to Congress. The president must also name the specific statutory provisions he or she plans to use. If no law connects an action to an emergency, the declaration cannot supply that power.

One widely used example is the International Emergency Economic Powers Act of 1977. Once an emergency is declared over a foreign threat, that statute lets the president block assets and restrict financial transactions. Many U.S. sanctions programs rest on it. Without a declaration, those powers stay unavailable.

A declared emergency does not last indefinitely by default. It ends automatically after one year unless the president publishes a notice in the Federal Register continuing it. Presidents routinely file these renewals. Some emergencies have stayed in effect for decades.

Congress has its own way to end an emergency. Lawmakers can pass a joint resolution that terminates the declaration, and that resolution is subject to a presidential veto. The act originally allowed termination by concurrent resolution, which does not go to the president. After the Supreme Court struck down legislative vetoes in INS v. Chadha in 1983, Congress switched the mechanism to a joint resolution in 1985.

Whether a president can take a given step during a declared emergency turns on the underlying statute. Congress decides in advance which powers an emergency can unlock. The president decides when to declare one.

An executive order cannot make new law, impose a tax or fund a program Congress has not paid for. The Constitution gives those powers to Congress, and an order cannot transfer them to the president.

Article I opens by vesting "all legislative Powers herein granted" in a Congress made up of a Senate and a House of Representatives. The president's formal part in passing a bill comes after Congress acts, when he signs it or vetoes it. An order that tries to create a new legal duty for the public, with no statute behind it, steps outside the executive role.

Money follows the same logic. Article I, Section 9 says no money may be drawn from the Treasury except through appropriations made by law. That provision is known as the Appropriations Clause. It means the executive branch cannot spend federal funds simply because a president wants a project done.

The taxing power also sits with Congress. Article I, Section 8 lists the power to lay and collect taxes among the powers of Congress. Article I, Section 7 adds that bills for raising revenue must originate in the House. A president cannot create a new tax by signing an order.

What an order can do is steer money Congress has already provided. Appropriations laws often leave agencies room to choose among eligible projects or to set priorities within a funding stream. A president can use an order to direct how agencies use that discretion. The direction must stay inside the amount, purpose and time period Congress set.

Federal statutes reinforce these constitutional limits. The Antideficiency Act bars federal officials from spending or committing money beyond what Congress has appropriated. Officials who break it can face administrative discipline. Knowing and willful violations can also carry criminal penalties.

The Impoundment Control Act of 1974 addresses the reverse situation, in which a president wants to hold back money Congress has approved. Under that law, a president may propose canceling funds, a request known as a rescission. If Congress does not approve the proposal within 45 days of continuous session, the money must be made available for spending.

Together, these rules set the boundary. An executive order can organize, prioritize and direct the work of federal agencies. Creating a program, a tax or a new source of funding requires a bill that passes both chambers and becomes law.

An executive order that conflicts with the Constitution has no legal force. Presidential directives rank below the Constitution, and a court can declare an order invalid when it violates a constitutional right or limit.

That rule follows from the document's own structure. Article VI makes the Constitution the supreme law of the land. Article II requires each president to take an oath to preserve, protect and defend it. A directive from the same office cannot outrank the text its author swore to uphold.

The Bill of Rights adds specific limits. The Fifth Amendment bars the federal government from depriving any person of life, liberty or property without due process of law. An executive order is federal action. That guarantee applies to it just as it applies to a statute passed by Congress.

No order can amend the Constitution either. Article V requires an amendment to be proposed by two-thirds of both houses of Congress or by a convention called at the request of two-thirds of the states. Ratification then takes three-fourths of the states. The president has no formal role in that process.

A written limit does not always stop an order when it is issued. Executive Order 9066 is a cautionary example. President Franklin Roosevelt signed it in February 1942. It authorized the removal of Japanese Americans from the West Coast.

The Supreme Court upheld the use of that order in Korematsu v. United States in 1944.

The Court repudiated Korematsu in Trump v. Hawaii in 2018. The majority opinion in that case stated that Korematsu was wrong the day it was decided.

The sequence shows that a ruling in an order's favor is not necessarily the final judgment on its constitutionality. A later Court can reject the earlier reasoning outright. The Constitution's limits apply to every executive order, whichever president signs it.

Federal courts can strike down an executive order that goes beyond the president's legal authority. The leading case is Youngstown Sheet & Tube Co. v. Sawyer. On June 2, 1952, the Supreme Court ruled 6–3 that President Harry Truman lacked the power to seize the nation's steel mills during the Korean War.

The case grew out of a labor dispute. In April 1952, the United Steelworkers union was preparing to strike. Truman feared a shutdown would cut off steel needed for weapons and ammunition in Korea. He issued Executive Order 10340. It directed Secretary of Commerce Charles Sawyer to take possession of the mills and keep them running.

The steel companies sued. They argued that no statute and no clause of the Constitution allowed the president to take private property this way. The government countered that the president held inherent power to act in a national emergency. It also pointed to his role as commander in chief.

Justice Hugo Black wrote the majority opinion. He held that the president's power to issue an order must come from an act of Congress or from the Constitution itself. Neither supported the seizure. Black rejected the idea that the commander-in-chief role let the president take private property to keep a labor dispute from halting production. That kind of policy choice, he wrote, belonged to Congress.

Truman complied with the ruling and returned the mills to their owners. Chief Justice Fred Vinson wrote the dissent, joined by Justices Stanley Reed and Sherman Minton.

Justice Robert Jackson wrote a concurring opinion that has outlasted the case itself. He sorted presidential action into three tiers. Power is at its strongest when the president acts with authorization from Congress. It falls into an uncertain middle zone when Congress has said nothing. It is at its weakest when the president acts against the expressed or implied will of Congress.

Jackson placed the steel seizure in the third tier. Congress had considered seizure as a tool for labor emergencies and chose other methods instead. Those methods included the procedures in the Taft-Hartley Act of 1947.

Courts still use Jackson's framework when an executive order is challenged. The Supreme Court relied on it in Dames & Moore v. Regan in 1981, a case over the agreements that ended the Iran hostage crisis. It applied the framework again in Medellín v. Texas in 2008. The test asks the same basic question in each case: what has Congress said, and does the president's action fit within it?

Congress can undo an executive order in three ways. It can pass a law that supersedes the order, change the statute the order relies on or cut off the money needed to carry it out. Each route requires a bill that clears both chambers and survives a possible presidential veto.

The most direct option is a superseding statute. An executive order ranks below an act of Congress. When the two conflict on a matter within Congress's constitutional authority, the statute controls. Lawmakers can write a bill that bars the policy an order sets out. They can also require agencies to act in a different way.

A second option targets the order's foundation. Many orders rest on power that Congress delegated through a specific statute. If lawmakers amend or repeal that statute, the delegated authority can narrow or disappear. An order built on it can then lose its legal footing, even if the new bill never names the order.

The third option uses the power of the purse. Congress can attach language to an appropriations bill stating that no funds may be spent to implement a particular order. An agency cannot carry out work that has no money behind it. Lawmakers can write these limits into the regular spending bills that fund the government.

Every one of these routes ends at the president's desk. Under Article I, Section 7 of the Constitution, a bill passed by both chambers goes to the president. The president can sign it or veto it. A president can veto a bill that would reverse one of his or her own orders.

The Constitution gives the president 10 days, Sundays excepted, to act on a bill. If Congress adjourns during that window and prevents the bill's return, the unsigned bill does not become law. This is known as a pocket veto. Congress has no chance to override it.

Overcoming a regular veto takes a two-thirds vote in both the House and the Senate. The Supreme Court held in Missouri Pacific Railway Co. v. Kansas that this means two-thirds of members present and voting, provided a quorum is present. When neither party holds that many seats, an override needs votes from both parties.

The legislative route works differently from a lawsuit. A court asks whether an order was lawful. Congress can change the law going forward, whether or not the order was valid when the president signed it.

An executive order binds the executive branch, not the public at large. Private citizens and state governments feel its effects indirectly, through what federal agencies do in response, such as issuing regulations, changing enforcement priorities or writing contract terms.

That limit follows from what an order is. It is an instruction from the president to the officials who carry out federal law. A cabinet department or a federal procurement office can be told what to do. A shop owner, a landlord or a commuter is not part of that chain of command.

The route to the public usually runs through an agency. An order might direct a department to draft a rule on a particular subject. Under the Administrative Procedure Act of 1946, many such rules go through public notice and comment before they take effect. Once finalized, the rule can carry legal force for individuals and companies.

In that case, the obligation on a private person comes from the regulation and the statute behind it. The order supplied the direction. It did not, by itself, create a duty for anyone outside government.

States stand on different footing. The Tenth Amendment reserves to the states, or to the people, the powers the Constitution neither delegates to the federal government nor denies to the states. States are separate sovereigns with their own legislatures, courts and executives.

A president cannot order a state legislature to pass a law, repeal one or adopt a policy. Governors do not report to the White House.

In Printz v. United States (1997), the Supreme Court held that the federal government cannot command state officers to administer or enforce a federal regulatory program. That case involved an act of Congress. The principle limits the federal government as a whole, not only the president.

Federal money is another route to influence states, and it has its own limits. Conditions attached to federal funding for states must be authorized by Congress. A president cannot add new strings to state grants on presidential authority alone.

In South Dakota v. Dole (1987), the Supreme Court upheld a law tying a portion of federal highway funds to states setting a minimum drinking age of 21. That condition came from Congress and was written into statute.

The practical test of an order's reach is whom it addresses. When the answer is a federal agency, the public feels the effect only after that agency acts within the authority the law gives it.

Yes. A president can revoke, amend or replace any executive order by signing a new one. That power belongs equally to the president who issued the original and to every successor who follows.

The reason is structural. An executive order is not a statute. It is a directive from the head of the executive branch to the people who work in it. Whoever holds that office can change the directive. A new president does not need the consent of the predecessor who signed the original order.

The new order does the work. It typically names the earlier order by number and states that it is revoked in whole or in part. A partial revocation can strike specific sections while leaving the rest in force. The replacement order is published in the Federal Register like any other. That gives the public a written record of what changed and when.

A change in administration does not end an order on its own. An executive order stays in force after the president who signed it leaves office. Unless its own text sets an end date or a condition for expiring, it does not lapse with the passage of time. An order that no successor revisits can remain in effect across several administrations.

Revocation also looks forward, not backward. Actions taken lawfully while an order was in effect stay valid after it is withdrawn. A contract signed, a policy carried out or a decision made under the old order is not erased by the new one.

Rules that agencies formally adopted to carry out an order can be harder to unwind. A final regulation issued through notice-and-comment rulemaking generally has to be rescinded through a similar process. A new executive order can direct an agency to begin that work. It cannot delete the regulation by itself.

Statutes sit on firmer ground. A law passed by Congress and signed by a president can be repealed only by Congress. A policy first set by executive order becomes harder to reverse if lawmakers later write it into a statute.
