How Senate confirmations work, from nomination to final vote

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The Senate confirmation process runs from nomination to final vote, and these 10 rules and stages explain how presidential nominees win or lose approval

A three-word phrase in the Constitution — "advice and consent" — gives the Senate a say over who runs federal departments and who sits on federal courts. It also covers who represents the U.S. abroad. The President picks the nominee. The Senate decides whether that pick takes office. That division of power gives two branches of government a role in every appointment that requires confirmation.

The Senate confirmation process is not a single vote. It is a chain of formal rules, committee customs and floor procedures. Each link can move a nominee forward or hold one back. Some steps are written into the Constitution. Others exist only in the standing rules of the Senate. A few are traditions that a committee chair can honor or set aside.

Those rules have not stayed fixed. The threshold for ending debate on nominees has dropped from a supermajority to a simple majority. The time allowed for debate after that vote has been cut for many lower-level posts. The 2013 and 2017 changes were each led by the Senate majority leader of the day.

This guide follows the path a nominee takes. It starts with the constitutional text and the kinds of jobs that need Senate approval. It then covers the questionnaires, financial disclosures and background checks that come before any hearing. Next come the committee hearing and committee vote. After that is the blue slip custom, which gives home-state senators a voice on some judicial and law enforcement picks.

From there the guide moves to the floor. It explains holds, cloture votes and the final tally, including what happens when the Senate splits evenly. The ninth section looks at nominations that fail, stall or get sent back to the White House. The 10th covers recess appointments and the bulk approval of military promotions. Both work outside the usual one-by-one vote.

Each section stands on its own. A reader who wants to know what a hold means, or why a vice president might cast the deciding vote, can go straight to that section. Together the sections show how a presidential choice becomes a confirmed official — and every point where that path can end.

Article II, Section 2 of the U.S. Constitution divides the power to fill top federal posts between two branches. The President nominates, and the Senate gives its advice and consent before an appointment is made.

The clause names specific categories of officials. It lists ambassadors, other public ministers and consuls, and justices of the Supreme Court. It then adds a broader category: all other officers of the U.S. whose appointments the Constitution does not otherwise provide for.

The order of the verbs matters. The President acts first by choosing a person and sending that name forward. The appointment comes only after the Senate has consented. A nomination on its own does not place anyone in office.

The phrase "advice and consent" is the constitutional footing for the Senate's role. Committee hearings, floor debate and confirmation votes on presidential nominees all rest on these words.

The same clause also limits how far Senate review reaches. It lets Congress decide that certain positions, which the text calls inferior officers, can be filled without the Senate's consent.

For those posts, Congress may vest the appointment power in one of three places. It can give the power to the President alone. It can give it to the courts. It can also give it to the heads of departments.

The decision rests with Congress rather than the President. Lawmakers determine which inferior offices skip Senate review and which of those three authorities makes the selection.

The result is a two-track system built into the same clause. Ambassadors, public ministers, consuls, Supreme Court justices and other officers not covered elsewhere go through both the President and the Senate. Inferior officers can go through a single appointing authority if Congress chooses that route.

Beyond those four named categories, the text does not list individual job titles. Which posts require the Senate's consent depends on the general category of officers and on the inferior-officer choices Congress makes.

Cabinet secretaries, ambassadors, federal judges, U.S. attorneys, U.S. marshals and senior military officers must all win Senate approval before taking office. These posts sit on the confirmed side of a line the Constitution draws between officers who need the Senate's consent and lesser officers who may not.

The roster spans the executive and judicial branches. Cabinet secretaries run the executive departments. Ambassadors represent the U.S. to foreign governments. Senior military officers lead the armed forces. Federal judges sit at three levels: district courts, circuit courts and the Supreme Court. Judges at all three levels need the Senate's consent.

U.S. attorneys and U.S. marshals belong on the list as well. Both posts are tied to individual federal judicial districts. A U.S. attorney leads federal prosecutions in a district. A U.S. marshal handles law enforcement work for the federal courts in a district. The Senate must sign off on both, just as it does for a Cabinet secretary or a judge.

Senate confirmation does not cover every federal job. The Constitution gives Congress a choice when it comes to inferior officers. Congress must make that choice by law. Lawmakers can place the power to appoint them with the President alone. They can also give it to the federal courts or to the heads of departments. When Congress takes one of those routes, the appointment does not go to the Senate.

Without a law vesting an inferior officer's appointment elsewhere, the default under the Constitution is nomination by the President with the Senate's advice and consent.

The result is a two-tier system. A nominee for a senior post, such as a Cabinet secretary or a federal judge, faces the Senate's full process before taking the job. That process runs from vetting through a committee review to a floor vote. An inferior officer whose appointment Congress has assigned to the President alone, a court or a department head does not.

A presidential nomination becomes official when the White House sends a formal nomination to the Senate. The Senate then refers it to the committee with jurisdiction over the post, where questionnaires, financial disclosure and background checks begin.

The formal nomination turns a president's choice into business the Senate can act on. Until that document arrives, senators have no pending nomination to consider. Once it does, the nomination does not go straight to the full chamber. It goes to a committee first.

Referral depends on the type of job. The Senate Judiciary Committee handles nominations for federal judges. The Senate Foreign Relations Committee handles ambassadors. The Senate Armed Services Committee handles military officers. The position, not the person nominated, decides which committee reviews the pick.

Vetting begins with paperwork. Nominees complete questionnaires for the committee reviewing them. Those documents put a written record of the nominee in front of senators before any questioning takes place.

Executive-branch nominees face an added financial step. People picked for posts in federal departments and agencies file financial disclosure. That disclosure is reviewed through the Office of Government Ethics. A nominee's finances are therefore on file before the Senate weighs the nomination.

Judicial nominees undergo an FBI background check on top of their questionnaires. That check covers people nominated to the federal bench, where judges are appointed to the courts rather than to an executive agency.

These requirements produce different paths for different posts. An ambassador nominee goes to Foreign Relations and, as an executive-branch pick, files financial disclosure reviewed through the Office of Government Ethics. A judicial nominee goes to Judiciary and undergoes an FBI background check. A nominee for a senior military post goes to Armed Services.

All of this happens before the committee holds a hearing or takes a vote. The questionnaires, disclosure filings and background checks form the record that committee members review once the nomination moves to that next stage.

A Senate committee reviews a nominee in two stages: it holds a hearing, and then it votes on how to report the nomination. That vote shapes what comes next, but it does not settle the outcome.

The hearing comes first. The nominee appears before the committee with jurisdiction over the post. Senators on that panel question the nominee directly before any vote takes place.

The two stages do different work. The hearing lets panel members put their questions to the nominee in person. The vote then asks those same members to record a position on the nomination.

The committee votes after the hearing ends. The question on the table is not whether the nominee gets the job. It is how the nomination will be reported to the rest of the chamber.

A committee has three options. It can report a nomination favorably. It can report it unfavorably. It can also report it without recommendation. Whichever option the panel picks, the nomination leaves the committee and heads to the floor.

Each option tells senators outside the panel something different. A favorable report signals that the committee backs the nominee. An unfavorable report signals that the committee opposes the pick. A report without recommendation moves the nomination along while the committee takes no position.

Only committee members take part in that vote. Its result reflects the judgment of one panel, not the view of the Senate as a whole. The floor is where the full chamber first weighs in.

None of these reports binds the chamber. A favorable report does not guarantee confirmation. An unfavorable one does not end a nomination. On the floor, any senator can vote, whether or not he or she sits on the committee.

The committee stage informs the decision rather than making it. The full Senate decides whether the nominee takes office.

The blue slip is a Senate Judiciary Committee custom that lets the two senators from a nominee's home state weigh in before the committee acts. It is a committee practice, not a rule of the full Senate.

The tradition works through consultation. When a nomination tied to a particular state reaches the Judiciary Committee, the senators who represent that state are asked for their view. Their answer signals whether they are willing to see the nomination move forward in committee.

The practice reaches beyond the courts. Under Chairman Chuck Grassley, the committee honored blue slips for district judges and for U.S. attorneys. For those posts, the views of home-state senators carried real weight in whether a nominee advanced. A senator who declined to return a favorable slip could keep that nomination from moving through the committee.

Circuit judges were treated differently. Starting in 2017, the committee did not apply the blue slip to circuit court nominees. A home-state senator who withheld support could not use the custom to hold up a circuit nomination in committee.

The split shows how a custom differs from a rule. The full Senate does not vote on the blue slip, and its standing rules do not require it. The committee chair decides how much weight the slips carry and for which kinds of nominees. That is why district and circuit nominees could be handled differently by the same committee.

A rule of the full Senate applies the same way no matter who leads a committee. A committee custom carries no such guarantee. A later chair can draw the lines in a different place, widening or narrowing the set of posts where home-state objections count.

The blue slip operates only inside the committee stage. It can shape whether a nomination advances in Judiciary. It does not replace the committee hearing, the committee vote or the final decision on the Senate floor.

A Senate hold is an informal notice from a senator that he or she intends to object if leaders try to move a nomination by unanimous consent. A hold can slow a nominee down, but it does not block a nomination outright.

The hold works through unanimous consent, the shortcut the Senate uses to skip formal procedural steps. Under unanimous consent, a matter moves forward only if no senator objects. A single objection is enough to stop the shortcut.

That power belongs to every member of the chamber. The objecting senator does not need to sit on the committee that reviewed the nominee. Any senator can refuse consent on the floor, regardless of party or seniority.

A hold announces that objection in advance. The senator does not have to wait for the nomination to reach the floor. Leaders learn ahead of time that consent will be refused. They can then decide whether to take the formal route instead.

The hold is not a vote, and it does not formally reject anyone. It is a statement of intent. Its force comes entirely from the right every senator already has to deny unanimous consent.

The majority still has a formal route around an objection. It can file for cloture, the procedure that brings debate on a nomination to a close. Once the majority takes that route, the nomination can advance without the objecting senator's agreement.

The practical effect of a hold is to force the slower path. A nomination that could have cleared the chamber by consent must go through formal procedure. That procedure includes a separate cloture vote before the final vote on confirmation. Every extra step uses floor time that unanimous consent would have saved. The decision shifts from a single senator's objection back to the majority.

A hold gives one senator influence over timing, not a veto over the nominee.

Cloture is the Senate procedure that ends debate on a nomination. For every type of nominee, it now takes a simple majority rather than 60 votes. Senate Rule XXII governs the process. Rule changes in 2013 and 2017 lowered the threshold, and a 2019 change shortened how long debate can run once cloture passes.

The Senate does not cut off discussion of a nominee by default. Until debate on a nomination formally ends, the chamber cannot move to a confirmation vote. A successful cloture vote closes that discussion and puts the nominee on a path to the final tally.

Before 2013, ending debate on a nomination required 60 votes. Under that threshold, a nominee with the backing of a majority could still stall if supporters could not reach 60. The minority party therefore held real leverage over which nominees reached a vote at all.

In 2013, Majority Leader Harry Reid led a change that set a simple-majority threshold for cloture on executive-branch nominations and lower-court judges. Supreme Court nominees were left out of that change. They still faced the 60-vote bar.

That exception did not last. In 2017, Majority Leader Mitch McConnell extended the simple-majority threshold to Supreme Court nominees. The Senate then confirmed Neil Gorsuch to the Supreme Court on April 7, 2017, by a vote of 54-45.

Passing cloture does not trigger an immediate confirmation vote. Senate rules allow a period of post-cloture debate first. In 2019, the Senate cut that window to two hours for most sub-Cabinet executive nominees and district judges.

Higher-level posts kept a longer runway. Up to 30 hours of post-cloture debate remains available for Cabinet-level nominees, circuit judges, Supreme Court justices and certain other positions. The gap shapes the floor schedule. The two-hour limit lets the chamber process lower-level nominees faster than the 30-hour maximum allowed for top posts.

A nominee is confirmed when a simple majority of the senators voting approves the nomination. If those senators split evenly, the vice president can break the tie. That power comes from Article I, Section 3 of the Constitution.

The phrase "senators voting" carries weight. The threshold is measured against the votes actually cast on the floor, not against the full membership of the chamber. A senator who is absent or does not vote is left out of the count. As a result, the number of yes votes needed to win can change from one roll call to the next.

A tie means neither side has a majority, and the nomination has not been approved. Article I, Section 3 gives the vice president a way to resolve that deadlock. The vice president's vote is then added to the final tally. That is why a tiebreak confirmation shows up as a one-vote margin.

Vice President Mike Pence used that power on Feb. 7, 2017. He broke a tie to confirm Betsy DeVos as Education Secretary. The recorded count was 51-50. Senators had divided 50-50 on the nomination, and Pence cast the deciding yes vote.

Two recent Supreme Court confirmations show the simple-majority standard working without a tiebreaker. Amy Coney Barrett was confirmed on Oct. 26, 2020, by a vote of 52-48. Ketanji Brown Jackson was confirmed on April 7, 2022, by a vote of 53-47.

In each case, more senators voted yes than voted no. That is all the rule requires. Neither count was tied. The vice president had no role in either outcome. Barrett won by four votes and Jackson by six.

The final vote is the Senate's actual decision on a nomination. Committee action, holds and cloture all come earlier in the process. None of them settles whether a nominee is confirmed. Only the floor vote does that.

A nomination can fail in three ways. The Senate can vote it down, decline to act on it, or let it go back to the President under the chamber's own rules. Each path ends a nomination without a confirmation, and each works through a different mechanism.

An outright rejection happens on the Senate floor. The full chamber takes a recorded vote, and more senators vote no than yes. Robert Bork's Supreme Court nomination ended this way in 1987, when the Senate voted 58-42 against him. The roll call put every senator's position on the record.

Cabinet nominees can meet the same end. In 1989, the Senate rejected John Tower as Defense Secretary by a vote of 53-47. A floor defeat is not limited to judicial nominees. A nominee to lead an executive department faces the same final test as a nominee to the high court.

A stalled nomination fails without any vote at all. Merrick Garland was nominated to the Supreme Court in March 2016. The Senate never held a hearing on Garland, and it never held a vote. His nomination produced no committee record and no floor tally. The Senate's role in appointments includes the option not to act.

The third path is procedural. Senate Rule XXXI governs nominations that are still pending when the chamber stops meeting for an extended stretch. If the Senate adjourns or recesses for more than 30 days, those nominations go back to the President. The Senate can prevent that by agreeing, through unanimous consent, to keep them in place.

A returned nomination is no longer before the Senate. No further committee or floor action can take place on it.

The unanimous consent requirement gives each senator leverage at this stage. One objection is enough to stop the Senate from holding pending nominations through a long break. That leaves the fate of those nominations outside the majority's sole control.

These outcomes separate a nomination that is defeated from one that runs out of time. A defeat requires more no votes than yes votes on the floor. A stall or a return requires only that the process stop before any final vote.

Two routes let a nominee take office without a separate, individual Senate roll call. Recess appointments, authorized by Article II, Section 2, Clause 3 of the Constitution, let the President fill vacancies while the Senate is away. Military promotions, in most cases, clear the Senate in bulk by unanimous consent.

The recess appointment power works differently from the standard nomination path. Under the ordinary process, a nominee moves through committee and then the floor before taking office. A recess appointment skips those steps while the Senate is not in session. The President acts alone, and the appointee can begin serving without a confirmation vote.

The central legal question is what counts as a recess. The Supreme Court addressed it in NLRB v. Noel Canning, decided in 2014. The justices held that a Senate recess lasting fewer than 10 days is presumptively too short for the recess appointment power. Under that standard, a break of a few days generally does not qualify.

The ruling put the Senate's calendar at the center of the issue. If the chamber never stays away long enough, the President has no qualifying recess to use. The Senate has used pro forma sessions to avoid long recesses. These are brief sessions held for form's sake during a break. They divide what would otherwise be one long absence into shorter stretches.

Officer promotions follow their own track on the Senate floor. Rather than hold a separate vote for each officer, the Senate groups promotions together. A single unanimous consent request can then clear the whole list.

That shortcut depends on every senator going along. The bulk method covers most military promotions, not every one. A nomination left out of the routine package has to move through the Senate's regular procedures instead.

The two routes rest on different foundations. Recess appointments turn on the calendar and the 10-day line drawn in Noel Canning. Bulk military approvals turn on routine agreement among senators.

7 Soulslikes With the Most Nominations at The Game Awards

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Ronald is a video game journalist with more than 10 years of experience in the industry, working on several of the most important Spanish websites.

Since 2009, FromSoftware has established an entire movement thanks to its work with the Soulsborne philosophy, not only giving us some of the best games of recent generations but also sparking the creativity of numerous developers worldwide.

The emergence of Soulslikes as a significant variation of ARPGs has been remarkable, influencing everyone from independent developers to major studios to follow in the footsteps of its challenging yet memorable journeys.

As more and more projects have joined the growing trend, the genre's average quality has increased considerably, which has been reflected in the ceremonies that celebrate each year's best titles.

Therefore, to remember which experiences have reached the pinnacle of recognition within the interactive industry, I present to you this list of the seven Soulslikes with the most nominations at The Game Awards.

Being not only the first great Soulslike game but also the first title to be nominated for a Game Awards category, Nioh is one of the genre's greatest representatives for many reasons.

Although it lost the Best Action Game award in 2017 to Wolfenstein II: The New Colossus, Team Ninja took the first step in establishing what would eventually become the finest and most successful series in this subgenre.

Team Ninja was the first studio to place as much faith in the original formula as in its talent and expertise to create something uniquely its own while remaining instantly recognizable. The result was a devilishly challenging title with a remarkable personality that resonated with the community thanks to its profound experience.

The sequels would improve upon what was already quite enjoyable, although it's clear that the first Nioh is the installment with the most historical significance and contribution.

After 2017, we would have to wait three years before another Soulslike was represented at The Game Awards, which finally happened with Mortal Shell's nomination for Best Debut Game.

Following Nioh's unfortunate trajectory, Cold Symmetry's project fell victim to the Phasmophobia phenomenon, though its achievement's magnitude was still considerable, not only given its independent status but also its significance in a niche genre.

Even without reaching its most refined version, which would come with the sequel, the title already possessed enough authority and ambition to be featured at the industry's most prestigious awards ceremony, a truly remarkable feat.

Currently, Mortal Shell might seem slightly outdated, more so considering the comprehensive improvements offered by its successor, yet this shouldn't detract from the fact that it was one of the best-received Soulslikes upon its release.

A list of ten soulslike games where the player turns out to be the villain by the end.

For the second time in a row, Team Ninja had to watch the Best Action Game award slip through their fingers, with the category going to Hades instead of Nioh 2.

Despite the comprehensive improvements the sequel added, it wasn't able to secure more submissions than the one it had received years earlier, partly because many preferred the original's darker, more focused, and less cluttered style.

Regardless of the reasons, and even though it was among 2020's standout titles, the studio had to settle for repeating the milestone, though it's more significant than it might seem considering that the IP now accounted for two-thirds of the genre's all-time nominations.

Certainly, two out of three is nominally a small number, but for a type of game that, by definition, will never achieve mainstream success, it's a clear demonstration of the IP's stature.

The genre suffered another defeat in 2021 with Death's Door, a practically 'Soulslite' approach that, nevertheless, remains one of the current decade’s best action-adventure titles.

In the Best Independent Game category, Acid Nerve's creation succumbed to Kena: Bridge of Spirits, but it did so surrounded by absolute marvels of the indie ecosystem, such as Inscryption and Loop Hero, demonstrating that the level of competition was extremely high.

Death's Door, like Hollow Knight in 2017, belongs to that lighter variant of the genre that focuses on concepts and ideas rather than strict mechanics, and the result was a hybrid between The Legend of Zelda and Dark Souls that feels truly majestic.

It may not have won, but Titan Souls' creators made a tremendous leap in quality by moving from the iconic boss rush to this fully-fledged adventure, which continues to be a regular fixture on lists of the best experiences of its kind.

From the indie ecosystem to productions approaching AAA measures, these are ten Soulslike franchises that should come back by 2027.

Remnant: From the Ashes surprised the world with its magnificent combination of Soulslike mechanics, third-person shooter gameplay, and multiplayer, while Remnant 2 refined the package and capitalized on that success.

In 2023, the Best Action Game category once again featured a genre standard-bearer, though it had to face the bittersweet reality of defeat, particularly at the hands of FromSoftware and Armored Core 6: Fires of Rubicon.

However, after a 2022 in which only FromSoftware made an appearance at the event, returning to the biggest stage in video games was important enough to maintain the genre's vitality.

Remnant 2's success is undeniable, but so are the frustrations of a genre that, after more than five years of accumulating nominations, had yet to secure even a single award that would elevate it to a new level.

Lies of P made Soulslike history in 2023 by becoming the first game in the subgenre to receive more than one nomination, an achievement unmatched until then.

Round8 Studio and NEOWIZ emerged from absolute obscurity and yet released what I consider the best Soulslike ever made, not only perfectly emulating FromSoftware's work but also imbuing it with a recognizable and well-developed identity.

It lost Best RPG to Baldur's Gate 3 and Best Art Direction to Alan Wake 2, but considering that both were Game of the Year nominees and consistently rank among the decade's best games, there's little to criticize about Lies of P.

Unfortunately, the game's quality and refinement remain an anomaly that exceeds the genre's actual average, though it continues to be a benchmark for future Soulslikes aspiring to such heights.

There's nothing more fitting for this season than being destroyed by the same boss fifty-seven times in a row.

However, the biggest historical event for Soulslikes at The Game Awards came in 2024 with Black Myth: Wukong, by far the most acclaimed game in the genre from the specialized press.

With the highest number of nominations ever recorded at The Game Awards, the genre's only Game of the Year nomination, and the sole winner of a medal (two, actually), Game Science shattered all previous standards for Soulsborne heirs.

Its nominations for GOTY, Best Game Direction, Best Art Direction, Best Action Game, and Players' Voice were historic, as were its conquests over the latter two categories, cementing its place in the annals of Soulslikes.

One of its victories was in the popular vote's realm, the particular category decided entirely by users and not the specialized media, although it doesn't diminish the merit of a production that paved the way for a whole country.

Since its resounding success, China has opened up beyond the mobile market to continue capitalizing on Black Myth: Wukong's prosperity, positioning it as a giant whose shoulders carry an entire ecosystem thanks to its distinctive excellence.

If you're looking for the heir to Soulsborne games, look no further than this list of ten Soulslikes that are unexpected masterpieces.

‘Ink’ Star Jack O’Connell on His ‘New Era’ as a Leading Man: ‘Thank F**k for Danny Boyle’

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Jack O’Connell was feeling a little bashful. Seated outside for coffee, taking in the fresh fall air at the remote Salamander Resort in Middleburg, Virginia, the actor revealed that communication between him and Oscar-winning filmmaker Danny Boyle began long before he was cast as the lead in the director’s latest film, “Ink.”

“I used to write to Danny a lot. I wrote all the directors that I admire,” said the 36-year-old star from Derbyshire, England. “Not cap in hand, trying to get anything out of them, just letters of appreciation, lovely stuff that’s affected me and inspired me. ‘I hope we get to do it someday.’ And, in Danny’s case, we did.” Related Stories How Casting the New ‘Harry Potter’ Helped ‘Club Kid’ Find Its Adorable 10-Year-Old Star Casey Bloys to Receive the Milestone Award at 2027 PGAs

Technically “Ink,” in which O’Connell plays influential newspaper editor Larry Lamb after he’s recruited by outcast media magnate Rupert Murdoch to take over The Sun tabloid in 1969, is the second time the actor has worked with Boyle. The pair first officially met when he was brought in to play antagonist Jimmy Crystal in last year’s “28 Years Later,” directed by Boyle, and more prominently in January’s “28 Years Later: The Bone Temple,” directed by Nia DaCosta.

“It was right before I went and shot ‘Sinners,’” he said, referencing another major turn as a villain that loomed prominently over cinema this year. “The initial meeting wasn’t an audition. I just didn’t have the role. It was a meeting discussing the role with Danny and Nia.” It was the first time Boyle had acknowledged O’Connell, though he had a sneaking suspicion that he had been on the director’s radar for a while, having starred on the same iconic British teen drama “Skins” from which Boyle had found the star of his Best Picture winner “Slumdog Millionaire.”

“Listen, he cast Dev [Patel]. He knew about ‘Skins.’ I think his daughter put him onto ‘Skins’ in the first place. So whether or not he stuck it out for our generation, I don’t know,” said O’Connell.

It’s been almost two decades since he first played fan favorite Cook as part of the second generation of the trailblazing E4 series, which ran from 2007 to 2013. “Skins” still stands out as an experience that helped shape his ethos as an actor. “I certainly felt like we were on the coolest show around. It was so amazingly written. The directors we had were brilliant. The whole model of the way that you audition en masse and then they find their characters. I love that,” said O’Connell. “It’s really quite pure, isn’t it? People are just getting the roles because of ability.”

The “Ink” star first trained as an actor at a free program in Nottingham called the Television Workshop, which eventually helped lead to his first breakout film role in the coming-of-age drama “This Is England.” However, it was “Skins” that felt like a “crash course” continuing education. “You’re doing all the heavy stuff, the tough stuff, the really laborious side of doing a TV show like that. But also the fun and the joy that gets provoked by all the art, the amazing writing and everything else,” said O’Connell. He’s quick to remind that notable “Skins” alumni include not just Patel, but also writers like Emmy winner Jack Thorne and Tony-nominated playwright Lucy Kirkwood.

But free programs like the Television Workshop are increasingly hard to come by, meaning there are less and less egalitarian-type opportunities in the United Kingdom for an actor from a working-class background like O’Connell’s to find their own “Skins.” Asked what he would tell the next generation of artists, the “Ink” star said, “I wouldn’t know what advice I would give nowadays, which is a shocking indictment.”

What attracted O’Connell to playing Lamb in “Ink,” a role that has brought him to the Middleburg Film Festival to receive the Spotlight Actor Award, was the opportunity to address how the UK, and the world in general, reached this place of inequality. “We see this man just excavate his morals, and his soul, and his decisions, and this newspaper. Being the best-selling newspaper, he’s had to part with what makes people more human. To me, it’s a comment on capitalism,” said the star. “The beginning of hyper capitalism, post-war England, and individual opportunity versus looking after everyone else and your responsibility within society. That was all just getting ripped up.”

“Ink” is an adaptation of James Graham’s 2017 play of the same name, with Graham also serving as the screenwriter of Boyle’s film. Another major factor in O’Connell’s decision to play Larry Lamb was working with the playwright and the director, who had come from a similar background. With Boyle, “I came to his work as a fan first, before I knew I was going to try and be an actor,” he said. “I remember watching ‘The Beach,’ and that made me want to go and see the world. And watching ‘Trainspotting’ and that made me want to never take heroin. But also the amazing soundtrack that’s in there and the fucking unforgettable performances.”

With “Ink” they were preparing to tell what O’Connell sees as a quintessentially British story, and “what [Danny’s] films have meant for British cinema and British culture as a result is undeniable. Thank fuck for Danny Boyle. He is an artist by every stretch of the word. I would argue that his films have perhaps meant the most to us as a collective growing up. His work at the Olympics opening ceremony makes you tingle to watch it. It is fucking monumental.”

Boyle is why O’Connell, an outsider to the film and TV industry in the same way Lamb was to the publishing industry, could still envision himself becoming an actor one day. “His style has been able to make you feel part of the film. It takes you along. You feel like you’re in the movie somehow. You don’t feel like an observer, like a spectator. They give you a feeling and mood as well,” he said.

As for Graham, “We’re not too far from each other in a particular pocket of the UK. And although my town and his town famously don’t get on — that’s all to do with football and Brian Clough and whatever else — but he is a dramatist. He’s also interested in analyzing culture and the culture around him and examining it,” said O’Connell. “He’s a great spokesman for under-representation in the industry at home because eight percent of the entire industry are from our background.”

The actor added, “Once he knew that I was somebody that could be trusted with his words, not someone who was going to try and fucking destroy them, we really hit it off.”

Here the three were, as what some would call a band of outsiders, telling a story about these insurgents to the distinguished Fleet Street, where the most esteemed newspapers like The Daily Mirror were published. Playing Lamb was a bit of an exercise in what would happen if O’Connell’s comeuppance led him toward the dark side. “The intoxication of power is something that really interests me. And Larry, he began as a socialist, voted Labour, celebrated socialist politicians. He left Fleet Street having been knighted by Margaret Thatcher. … Talk about excavation, a whole U-turn politics there, colluded with Rupert Murdoch in tearing up the unions and workers’ rights. Sold his soul,” said O’Connell.

“There’s a great contradiction with Larry that he begins as an outsider. Even within the duration of our film, he’s trying to become an insider. He wants to be part of that gang. In fact, that’s where he ended up, Sir Larry. So there’s a great contradiction in the man. So I can’t afford to go all the way ‘outsider with a chip on his shoulder,’” said the actor.

With “Ink” following the successes of “Sinners” and “28 Years Later” so quickly, O’Connell has a newfound feeling of security and sense of becoming that sought-after leading man. “It probably is my most mature role to date,” he said. “I look back on my career and I just feel so fortunate. There’s not a single regret. So if this is a new era, yeah, brilliant, bring it on. A lot of my heroes, a lot of their work that I really celebrate, were around my age, so maybe that is inspiring and gives me cause to kick on.”

Netflix will release “Ink” in select theaters across the United States on Friday, December 11. The film will debut on the streaming service on January 8, 2027.

Barbara Tfank Spring 2027 Ready-to-Wear

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

In the age of AI, Barbara Tfank is, refreshingly, all about craft. Unlike other designers who may have moved on, she’s stayed true to her passion for eveningwear. As such, she continues to seek out fabrics from European textile mills she’s used since day one. This season, she looked to her vast archive and found a few unused Bianchini Férier prints to repurpose for her spring collection. “The artist Raoul Dufy actually painted and designed the textiles for them, so they’re collectibles,” she explained of the lively, lush florals in vivid hues. “I was just so happy to see these in my archive because there’s nothing digital about them, it was all done by hand and just feels timeless.”

With the prints, she revamped some beloved Tfank styles like her signature off-the-shoulder gowns in new spring-ready organza. In addition to her retrained column gowns, she cut one dress a touch shorter than she tends to lean toward—now reaching to mid-shin—in a bid to cater to the emerging casual evening gown customer (think someone who might want to wear a pair of thong heels rather than a proper Manolo when going out).

Along those same lines, she explored a few casual styles, like the bra tops that have been everywhere on spring 2027 runways, hers made in a groovy Férier print and paired with wide-leg pants. Other options include an airy halterneck dress with a long train and a casual navy-and-white sundress, for which Tfank mentions the fabric was once used to create a custom coat for First Lady Michelle Obama. Talk about divine timing to bring the print back.

Corn fights back against a fungus – but its defense has a hidden weakness

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A corn smut fungus uses the plant’s own defense enzymes to release a hidden signal that activates a receptor and speeds fungal cell division.

Corn, like every plant, fights back when something breaks in. When a fungus pushes into its tissue, the plant sends out enzymes that chop up proteins, including the ones the intruder secretes.

For the fungus behind corn smut, that defense turns out to be exactly the signal it has been waiting for.

The plant’s enzymes cut a protein secreted by the fungus, releasing a signal that helps the invader detect that it has entered plant tissue.

That cut frees a short fragment that was folded away inside. The fragment switches on a receptor in the fungal cell membrane, and the fungus starts dividing faster.

The plant’s defense, in other words, tells the invader it has arrived.

Florian Altegoer, who leads a research group at the Institute of Microbiology at Heinrich Heine University Düsseldorf, led the work with lead author Gabriel Mendoza-Rojas, a member of his group.

Colleagues at the University of Göttingen, NC State University, Imperial College London, and the University of Groningen took part.

The receptor belongs to a family of proteins called G protein-coupled receptors, which work as sensors in a cell’s outer membrane and relay signals from outside to inside.

Humans have more than 800 of them, and they are the targets of about 40% of approved drugs. Fungal versions are far less understood.

“We have identified a previously unknown fungal GPCR and uncovered an unusual mechanism by which it is activated,” Altegoer said.

Mendoza-Rojas put the twist more bluntly.

“The fungus thus ironically utilizes enzymes from its host plant to detect that it has entered the tissue successfully,” he said.

The fungus, Ustilago maydis, infects maize plants and causes large galls, especially on the ears, where it makes its spores.

Two of its genes were already known to be essential: delete either one and the fungus produces almost no galls.

One of those genes makes a secreted protein called Pit2, which corn enzymes cut into a fragment that blocks those same enzymes.

The other made a membrane protein of unknown function. When the team searched the fungal genome for proteins with the seven-helix shape of a receptor, that protein was one of 34 hits. They renamed it Gpe1.

They put the receptor into baker’s yeast engineered to glow red whenever it fires. Intact Pit2 produced a weak glow. Fragments of Pit2 fully activated the reporter at a tenth of the dose.

The team narrowed the active fragment down to nine amino acids.

The team also grew crystals of Pit2 and mapped its shape, which helped explain why the intact protein activates the receptor only weakly.

Two copies lock together, and the nine-amino-acid stretch is buried in the seam between them. Only the plant’s enzymes can release it.

“The peptide is hidden inside Pit2, like treasure in a chest that only plant enzymes can open,” Mendoza-Rojas said.

“However, opening this chest is more like opening Pandora’s box: It releases a signal that benefits the fungus at the plant’s expense.”

The team then watched living fungal cells under a microscope while the fragment flowed past them.

Soon after, the receptor pulled back from the cell surface into the cell’s interior, a common response of a receptor that has been triggered.

The cells also budded sooner. One round of division took about 226 minutes without the fragment and about 167 minutes with it, close to an hour less.

Faster division fits what the fungus has to do. It pauses its cell cycle while it grows a thread into the plant, then restarts division once it’s inside.

A regulator called Clp1 is part of that restart. Cells carrying extra Gpe1 made about five times as much of the Clp1 gene’s messenger molecule, and the fragment pushed it higher still.

“Our work establishes a mechanistic link between sensing the host environment and resuming fungal proliferation after entry,” Altegoer said.

The receptor mattered in whole plants too. The team changed single building blocks in the receptor’s predicted binding pocket and infected corn seedlings with the altered fungi.

Two of the changes, which also crippled the receptor in yeast, sharply reduced the galls on seedlings scored 12 days later.

Gpe1 has relatives across the smut fungi, but Pit2 shows up only in the corn fungus’s close relatives. The team tested whether each receptor would respond to the other species’ signal.

The signal from a related corn fungus switched on the corn smut receptor. The signal from a barley smut did not, and the corn fragments only weakly activated the barley receptor.

The nine-amino-acid stretch itself has barely changed across those species, which the authors read as the receptor and its signal evolving together.

A structural search also turned up a broad family of related receptors across the fungal group that includes mushrooms and smuts.

One patch of Gpe1 resembles a switch found in a large class of human receptors, including the light sensor in the eye.

The picture of how the fragment sits in the receptor comes from a computer prediction, which the team used only to decide which parts of the protein to alter.

The two weakest receptor variants might also be less stable rather than worse at binding, and the authors wrote that they can’t yet separate those explanations.

The restart of cell division is also only partly explained. The increase in Clp1 messenger RNA after receptor activation offers a possible explanation.

And the plant’s enzymes cut Pit2 into more than one fragment. One blocks the plant’s defenses; the other switches on the receptor.

How much each contributes during a real infection has not been tested, and that is the next experiment the authors wrote has to be done.

Whether other fungi that infect crops use the same mechanism is the larger open question, and the team plans to look.

“We want to find out whether other fungi use comparable receptors to sense their surroundings and adjust their growth,” Altegoer said.

The full study was published in the journal Nature Communications.

Pacific Palisades’ Bay Theater to Reopen on Friday With Johnny Depp Comeback ‘Ebenezer’

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Skydance CEO David Ellison has championed the location as a symbol of his commitment to the community and theatrical exhibition.

Set your calendars: The Bay Theater, the long-shuttered legacy cinema at Palisades Village, is reopening on Friday.

The resurrection marks nearly two years after the 2025 wildfires ravaged the area. Alongside developer Rick Caruso, Skydance CEO David Ellison has championed the venue as something like a town square and a physical symbol of his commitment to theatrical exhibition (RIP the Paramount Decrees).

The theater is reopening just in time for the release of Paramount’s Ebenezer, the studio’s buzzy bet on Johnny Depp’s A-list rehabilitation positioning the actor for a potential Hollywood comeback. The cinema won’t just be showing Skydance titles either. Godzilla Minus Zero, You Can See Everything, Wild Horse Nine and The Cat in the Hat (Warner Bros. Pictures Animation) will also be playing over the weekend.

Paramount Pictures revealed the revival last month, announcing plans to bring first-run theatrical releases, classics and experiential events to the cinema. The location is operated by The Lot, a luxury dine-in theater chain. Ellison has called it a “place for the Pacific Palisades community to gather around the shared experience of cinema,” saying that the Bay is “woven into the fabric of this neighborhood.”

Don’t be surprised if the studio plans to make the Bay its central hub for screenings given Ellison’s proximity to the location.

Caruso has discussed how he envisioned Palisades Village as a more intimate space than his other megapopular properties, The Grove in Los Angeles or the Americana at Brand in Glendale. After the original Bay Theatre closed in 1978, the Pacific Palisades went roughly 40 years without a movie theater of its own, until Caruso revived the name as part of Palisades Village in 2018.

Each theater offers luxury seating — with adjustable foot, back and head rests — USB charging, personal dining tables with custom lighting and service buttons for in-seat ordering. The lobby features a full-service bar with a video wall.

The reopening is part of a broader revival of L.A.’s cinema scene amid this year’s blazing box office run. It includes Sony’s planned restoration of the Cinerama Dome and rehabilitation of the adjacent ArcLight Hollywood, Amazon’s lease of the Culver Theater and Netflix’s purchase of the Egyptian in Hollywood. The resurgence isn’t limited to blockbuster venues either. There’s the Jason Reitman-led return of the Village in Westwood, Quentin Tarantino’s takeover of the Vista in Los Feliz and Kristen Stewart’s resuscitations of the Highland Theatre.

Man Who Discovered Renaissance Church Facade Hidden in His House to Receive €1 M. Reward

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

When Juan Francisco León Catena bought an old house in southern Spain, he expected that a planned renovation would incur costs. What he came to learn was that a Renaissance church facade was hidden behind the walls—and that it came with a reward worth more than €1 million ($1.2 million).

The discovery dates to November 2016, when León Catena and his father began tearing down interior walls in a property he’d purchased in Úbeda, a historic town in Andalusia that, together with neighboring Baeza, was designated a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 2003. Behind the plaster and partitions, they found an enormous stone facade measuring roughly 49 feet wide and 36 feet tall. Related Articles Three Works of Sacred Art Stolen From Spanish Churches as Authorities Warn of Rise in Art Theft $1.98 M. Worth of Gold Pieces Stolen From the Treasure of Villena

The stonework turned out to be part of the chapel of the former Hospital of San Jorge, also known as the Hospital of Pero Almindes, which was founded in 1347. The surviving facade includes Renaissance additions attributed to Andrés de Vandelvira, the 16th-century architect responsible for some of the region’s most celebrated buildings.

According to El País, heritage officials who examined the discovery praised its remarkable state of preservation and artistic value, describing it as a rare example of Renaissance funerary chapel architecture.

The discovery also brought León Catena’s renovation plans to a halt. Under Spanish heritage law, the facade is protected, meaning any alterations to the property are subject to strict restrictions. But the same legislation, passed in 1985, also provides financial rewards for people who accidentally discover historically significant objects or structures during construction or demolition work.

Getting that reward proved more complicated than finding the facade.

The regional government initially refused to pay, setting off a legal battle that dragged on for years. Spain’s Supreme Court has now settled the dispute in León Catena’s favor. Now, nearly a decade after the discovery, an Andalusian government valuation committee has determined that León is entitled to more than €1 million. He has yet to receive the money.

The monument itself, meanwhile, remains in limbo. León Catena has been negotiating with local officials over a plan to expose the facade to the street, allowing residents and tourists to see it. In exchange, he has proposed that the city cover the cost of renovating the four-story building.

But time may be running out. A conservation report commissioned by the regional government warned that leaking roofs in adjoining municipal properties have caused water damage, rotting wooden beams, and mold. The deterioration, officials said, could eventually lead to a structural collapse.

“Every month that passes without action increases the concern,” León Catena told El País, adding that the authorities have been warned about the risks but have yet to agree on a solution.

For now, the facade is cordoned off, essentially as it was when León Catena discovered it in 2016. He and his family, who had originally hoped to make the building their home, have since moved into another building on the same street.

“Too much time has passed,” León Catena said. His priority now, he added, is to make the long-hidden monument accessible to the public.

Sony have cancelled Fairgame$, once again reminding us that chasing live service big bucks basically never works

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

You know, I'm sure that Sony had every intention of following through on their intention to release 10 live service games by 2026, as they mentioned way back in 2022. Well, as far as an intention that's designed to please shareholders can go, anyway. And now here we are, almost five years on from that comment that continues to haunt them with the news Fairgame$ has been cancelled.

This comes from a report from Bloomberg, who shared that, thankfully, there are no planned job cuts due to the game being cancelled. In a statement, Sony said that, "The studio will shift its focus to collaborating with other development teams across PlayStation Studios, while also exploring new creative opportunities, though didn't go into detail as to who those other teams are.

Bloomberg were also privy to a note shared with developers at Haven Studios from executives Sean Decker and John Doyle that follows a recent test of the game: "While the test demonstrated strong operational excellence, live ops readiness and community engagement, player feedback and retention results were mixed and fell short of the goals we had set. Combined with challenging market conditions in the PVP shooter genre, we have made the difficult decision to cease development on the game."

Sony have, notably, failed to capitalise on the live service trend; I'm sure I don't need to remind you about Concord. Hell, this isn't even taking the end of Destiny 2 into consideration, once a driving force of live service games, or how Marathon has struggled to pick up an audience in the way that Sony may want it to. You just can't force a game to be popular, especially in a genre like shooters.

For those that either missed or forgot about Fairgame$, it was announced back in 2023 and was supposed to be a "competitive modern heist game where you team up to break into exotic locations and steal the cargo," all while taking on security and other, real people. Gameplay was never formally revealed, only a CGI trailer, and was set to come to PC as well as PlayStation. Bloomberg also noted that the game had been reworked multiple times since the announcement and was set to be re-revealed with a new name. Ultimately, it looks like Katharine was a little generous with her timeline of six months.

PlayStation Officially Cancels Live-service Shooter Fairgames

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Sony's latest game cancellation adds to a long list of failed GaaS projects

Sony has canceled Fairgames (aka Fairgame$) three years after the live-service shooter was announced, Bloomberg reported on Tuesday. No one at its developer, Haven Studios, is getting laid off, and instead Haven will support other Sony Interactive Entertainment-owned studios, according to that report.

Farigames recently held a playtest and, according to an internal email reviewed by Bloomberg, feedback from that playtest didn't live up to PlayStation's expectations. "While the test demonstrated strong operational excellence, live ops readiness and community engagement, player feedback and retention results were mixed and fell short of the goals we had set," executives Sean Decker and John Doyle said in the email. With "challenging market conditions in the PVP shooter genre," Sony decided to cancel the game.

Fairgames reportedly had been reworked multiple times since its 2023 reveal. It would have been the debut game from Haven, which was founded in 2021 by former Assassin's Creed producer and Google Stadia Games and Entertainment head Jade Raymond, who has since left the studio. Haven was acquired by Sony in 2022.

Back in 2022, Sony announced a huge push for live-service games, aiming to release at least 10 by 2026. While games like Helldivers 2 and Gran Turismo 7 have been steady live-service hits, Sony's push has largely failed. Concord is the most notable misfire, as it was actually released before Sony reversed course and shut it down shortly after launch in embarrassing fashion.

A live-service God of War game, a multiplayer The Last of Us game, and many others were canceled before they could see the light of day. PlayStation studios working on live-service games, including London Studios, Firewalk Studios, and Bluepoint Games, were all shut down in recent years. In June, Sony laid off most of the Destiny team at Bungie, as "Destiny 2 fell short of expectations" with players. Recently, Horizon Hunters Gathering was reportedly delayed so it can be rebooted in response to negative player feedback (which sounds not too dissimilar to the Fairgames situation).

I Tried Keurig’s First-Ever Espresso Machine—Is It Worth Adding To Your Coffee Routine?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

I’ve owned a Keurig, I’ve owned a Nespresso, and I’ve spent way too much money on coffee shop runs in between. Needless to say, I’m an expert on the subject. So when I heard that Keurig was launching its first-ever espresso machine, my interest was piqued. Could the brand that turned single-serve coffee into the household standard successfully switch up its game?

Keurig’s latest and possibly greatest innovation—a two-in-one single-serve coffee maker and espresso machine called the Alta System—will turn your home kitchen into a coffee shop thanks to a multifunctional design that brews everything from regular coffee to espresso, cappuccinos, and lattes. It comes with a built-in milk frother and functions exclusively on the new plastic- and aluminum-free pods (sustainability, hallelujah!).

Here’s whether the Keurig Alta System lives up to the hype, how it compares to other machines, and whether it deserves a spot on your own kitchen coffee bar.

Let’s go a little more in-depth, shall we? The Keurig Alta System is the brand’s new single-serve coffee and espresso machine that does a lot more than your standard K-Cup brewer. Unlike the brand’s traditional lineup, which uses K-Cup pods, the Alta introduces an entirely new innovation: AltaRounds. Your machine can read a code on the AltaRounds and adjust its brew settings accordingly, whether that’s pressure, temperature, or the water-to-coffee ratio. (K-Cups are still compatible with the Alta).

The integrated milk frother is another major upgrade for Keurig, letting you make hot and cold foam.

The Keurig Alta System is $499.99, which, yes, is pricier than other Keurig models. However, classic espresso machines can run you upwards of $1,000.

As for the AltaRounds, they’re sold in boxes of eight, ranging from $9.99 to $10.99 depending on the variety. If you wanna break that up by coffee, it’s about $1.25 to $1.37 a cup. Not too shabby.

As someone who is impatient and terrible at reading directions (hello, your type B friend reporting for duty), I wasn’t overly optimistic I’d figure it out that quickly. But it’s actually an easy device to navigate, thanks to the convenient LED screen that walks you through the whole process.

I was immediately transfixed by the machine. I texted my boyfriend, “I’m in love with it,” before it even finished brewing my first coffee. The Alta feels so much chicer on my countertops, and the pods are smaller and store more easily. Once I finally took that first sip, I was ready to toss my old Keurig in the trash (respectfully).

The drip coffee and espresso shots taste like something straight from an award-winning barista. I love the flexibility to make a black coffee one day and an iced caramel latte the next. And the milk frother just takes the entire thing to the next level.

Listen, $500 for a coffee machine is a lot. And maybe because of that, this isn’t an upgrade you can make immediately. Still, the Keurig Alta System is worth the hype; if you can swap your classic single-serve maker for it, you should. It checks literally every box—it’s more aesthetic, more versatile, and more sustainable.

The only caveat is if you’re the type of person who exclusively drinks drip coffee. In that case, it might not be worth the splurge. But if you want to cosplay as a barista and whip up new creations, from frothy cappuccinos to iced lattes and espresso martinis, the Alta provides all the room to experiment (and it will ultimately save you money on those $10 coffee runs).

Ivory Coast vs Gambia - prediction, team news, lineups

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

By Joshua Cole | 10 Nov 2026 21:27, Last updated: 10 Nov 2026 21:28

Ivory Coast and The Gambia will be looking to maintain their perfect starts to the 2027 Africa Cup of Nations qualifying campaign when they meet at Stade de la Paix in Bouake on Thursday evening.

Both teams have collected six points from their opening two Group C matches, and Thursday's encounter represents the first of two meetings between the sides in five days, with the return fixture scheduled to take place in The Gambia on November 17.

Ivory Coast have made an encouraging start under returning head coach Herve Renard, who began his second spell in charge with two wins from two competitive matches before suffering a friendly defeat to Cameroon.

His return opened with a 2-0 victory over Ghana, with Malick Yalcouye opening the scoring before Franck Kessie converted a second-half penalty, and the Elephants followed that result with another 2-0 win against Somalia, as Bazoumana Toure and Yann Gboho found the net.

The Elephants are yet to concede in the qualifying campaign and will be hoping to maintain that defensive record against The Gambia on Thursday, having won their last four meetings with the Scorpions while scoring nine goals and conceding none.

Ivory Coast also boast an impressive home record in this fixture, having won all three previous meetings on their own soil, scoring eight goals without conceding.

While the Elephants have been particularly solid defensively at the start of this campaign, The Gambia's attacking firepower has been central to their own impressive opening, with the Scorpions scoring six goals across their first two qualifiers.

They began with a 2-1 victory over Somalia before producing an impressive comeback to beat Ghana 4-2 away from home, with Ebrima Colley scoring three goals across the two matches, including a brace against the Black Stars.

The West African side will be looking to make it three consecutive victories, a feat they have not achieved in the last five years, with that run predating Johnny McKinstry's appointment as head coach in June 2024.

However, one concern for the visitors will be their lack of defensive solidity, having failed to keep a clean sheet in their last five matches, although they have managed to score 10 goals during that period.

Gambia Africa Cup of Nations Qualifying form:

Amad Diallo has returned to the Ivory Coast squad after overcoming an injury concern, providing Renard with another attacking option for the double-header.

However, the Elephants will be without Odilon Kossounou and Guela Doue, who are both sidelined through injury, while Wilfried Singo, Simon Adingra, Evann Guessand and Sebastien Haller have not been included in the squad.

The Gambia have been boosted by the returns of Musa Barrow and Yankuba Minteh, who have both recovered from the injuries that kept them out of the September qualifiers.

Alieu Fadera and Adama Sidibeh are unavailable through injury, while James Gomez has not been included in the final squad.

Jesper Ceesay and Suleiman Camara have also withdrawn because of injuries, with Saidou Khan and Salieu Drammeh called up as their replacements.

United’s 1st Private-Jet-Like CRJ450 Set For December Debut In Chicago

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

United Airlines is preparing to introduce its first CRJ450 regional jets in December, with the aircraft expected to begin operating from Chicago O'Hare International Airport (ORD) and Denver International Airport (DEN) before the end of 2026, according to reporting from The Points Guy. The new configuration gives the 50-seat Bombardier CRJ200 a more premium cabin, including a dedicated first-class section, while retaining the smaller aircraft’s ability to serve markets that cannot support larger regional jets. The first aircraft, registered N973SW, was converted from a CRJ200 and is operated by SkyWest Airlines for United Express. Aviation Week confirmed that the first two conversions were still underway in early November.

The launch is part of United’s effort to improve service on shorter regional routes without relying exclusively on larger aircraft. Rather than introducing a new airframe, the CRJ450 repurposes an existing jet with a revised cabin, expanded luggage storage, and onboard Wi-Fi. That approach could give smaller communities access to a more comfortable United Express product, although the airline has not announced a complete list of destinations. Lafayette, Indiana, is one example of a smaller market that could be relevant to the aircraft’s role, but it should not be treated as a confirmed CRJ450 destination.

The CRJ450 retains the CRJ200 airframe but reduces passenger capacity from 50 to 41 seats. Its cabin includes 7 United First seats, 16 Economy Plus seats, and 18 standard economy seats. The reconfiguration creates a two-class product on an aircraft that previously operated with an all-economy layout.

United is also changing how passengers store their carry-on luggage. Overhead bins have been removed from the first-class cabin and replaced with a dedicated closet, giving passengers more open space around their seats. In economy, redesigned overhead bins are large enough to accommodate standard rollaboard bags, addressing a familiar inconvenience on smaller regional jets where larger carry-ons often have to be tagged and placed in the aircraft’s cargo hold.

The aircraft will also feature Starlink Wi-Fi, available free to MileagePlus members, according to United’s March 2026 announcement. However, the CRJ450 will not include the self-service snack bar found on United’s larger CRJ550. The result is a more upscale regional cabin, not a miniature version of a mainline aircraft.

The first CRJ450, N973SW, began life as a CRJ200 delivered in June 2004. Aircraft records from Planespotters list its conversion in March 2026, following its previous configuration as a 50-seat regional jet. United publicly unveiled the concept that same month, but completing the first aircraft has taken longer than subsequent conversions are expected to require.

SkyWest President and Chief Operating Officer Wade Steel told investors during the company’s third-quarter earnings call that the first two aircraft were still being converted. The work includes coordinating service bulletins with manufacturer support partners, with later modifications expected to take approximately two to three weeks per aircraft. Most of the work is being performed at SkyWest’s Tucson, Arizona, maintenance base.

The program is expected to expand substantially after the initial aircraft enter service. SkyWest anticipates converting approximately 50 CRJ200s into CRJ450s by 2028 and has a multi-year agreement with United to operate 40. The regional carrier also sees potential to retrofit additional aircraft used in its own prorate operations, with the broader opportunity potentially reaching 100 conversions. These figures describe different parts of SkyWest’s plans, rather than a confirmed United fleet commitment for 100 aircraft.

United’s 56-seat-economy 767 shows how far the airline is pushing premium.

United says the CRJ450 will primarily connect smaller cities with its hubs in Chicago and Denver. The aircraft’s reduced seating capacity gives the airline another way to offer first-class service in markets where demand may not justify a larger regional jet. It also provides more flexibility under the scope restrictions governing the number and size of regional aircraft that major US airlines can operate through their partners.

Lafayette, Indiana, could be an interesting example of the type of community that might benefit. Purdue University’s home airport has historically relied on limited regional service, and a smaller aircraft could make premium seating available on routes that would be difficult to support with a larger jet. However, United has not identified Lafayette as a planned destination, and the aircraft’s eventual assignments will depend on the airline’s network and commercial decisions. The CRJ450 also follows the strategy behind United’s CRJ550, which uses a modified CRJ700 airframe with fewer seats and a more premium cabin. The newer -450 applies a similar concept to the smaller CRJ200. For SkyWest, converting existing aircraft offers a way to extend the usefulness of its regional fleet while giving United another option for serving smaller markets.

The first aircraft’s December debut will therefore mark more than a cabin refresh. It will test whether United can make older 50-seat regional jets more commercially attractive by selling a better onboard experience, even with fewer seats available. If the initial conversions perform as intended, the CRJ450 could become a more common sight on United Express routes connecting smaller communities with Chicago and Denver over the next two years.

Avalanche’s price is testing the lower support zone around $8 to $8.20, analyst Gopal notes in one of his latest price analyses. A push below the mathematically-significant $9.29 resistance level would require greater buyer strength & a more consistent trading volume on Spot markets.

Taco Bell Made A Hot Dog With A Chalupa Bun &amp; Fans Are Horrified

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

“Imagine getting a glizzy from Taco Bell 😭”

Taco Bell is no stranger to controversial menu items, but one new viral dish might be the wildest yet.

Last week, the Tex-Mex chain launched the first-ever Live Más Open, a star-studded golf tournament hosted by Kevin Hart. The guest list for the nine-hole competition included pro athletes, television stars, musicians, and more. However, the appearance that’s generating the most buzz isn’t a celebrity—it’s a Taco Bell hot dog.

Attendees were served a lineup of event-exclusive dishes that we've never seen at the Bell before, like lemon pepper ranch-dusted chicken nuggets and pimento cheese Quesaritos. Taco Bell fans, though, have the most FOMO for the Fire Piña Hot Dog.

According to FoodBeast, the dish featured a hot dog served with the chain’s signature chalupa flatbread instead of a traditional bun. Taco Bell loaded the hot dog with grilled pineapple, cotija, pickled red onion, cilantro, and two different sauces. The exact sauces haven't been confirmed, although one attendee suspected they might be sour cream and Taco Bell Fire Sauce.

Bailey Fink from Allrecipes attended the Live Más Open and tasted the hot dog for herself. She called it “easily one of the best hot dogs I’ve ever had” from a fast-food restaurant and compared the flavor profile to a traditional Colombian hot dog (minus the potato chips).

Social media users who didn't get the chance to try the Fire Piña Hot Dog, though, don't seem too convinced. “The words Taco Bell and hot dog should not be in the same sentence,” one Instagram user wrote. Another added, “If you order a hot dog from Taco Bell, you need to be on watch list…”

But if you take a look at Taco Bell's origin story, introducing hot dogs would actually bring the chain full circle. Founder Glen Bell's early restaurants served hamburgers and hot dogs before he shifted his attention to tacos. He eventually opened Taco Tia, which preceded the first Taco Bell restaurant in Downey, California.

Unfortunately, though, it appears Taco Bell won't be adding hot dogs to the menu anytime soon. All the dishes served at the Live Más Open were made exclusively for the event, and the chain currently has no confirmed plans to roll them out nationwide.

But don't despair: Part of the golf tournament prize is the opportunity to help choose a discontinued menu item to bring back for a limited time next year. So while a Taco Bell hot dog likely isn't in our immediate future, we can still look forward to the return of an old favorite.

Lanterns Showrunner Responds To James Gunn's DC Departure Amid Season 2 Questions

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

An Internet User Just Made A Comment About James Gunn’s DC Tenure That Makes Me Feel Sad And Weird

Upcoming DC Movies: All The New Superhero Releases, Including James Gunn's Man Of Tomorrow

James Gunn Would Like You To Know He’s Not Trying To Recreate The MCU (Even With Superman)

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

In brightest day, in blackest night, is a renewal still in sight?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

DC’s Lanterns made quite a splash amid the 2026 TV schedule, as the HBO superhero drama received critical acclaim and strong viewership numbers. Since the show’s eight-episode season wrapped up in early October, fans have been wondering whether a second season might happen. Now, viewers may have even more questions about what’s going on, considering James Gunn is leaving his CEO post at DC Studios. Now, Lanterns showrunner Chris Mundy is weighing in on Gunn’s departure and Lanterns’ future.

As the head of DC Studios, Gunn was one of the driving creative forces behind Lanterns, and both he and co-CEO Peter Safran are credited as executive producers. Mundy, of course, worked with the pair to lay out the direction of the series. Mundy was recently interviewed by The Playlist and was directly asked about Gunn’s exit after the Paramount/Warner Bros. merger and whether it could spell the end of his superhero show. Mundy answered honestly but, sadly, his comments don’t inspire much confidence:

We thought everything had settled [after the merger]. I can imagine a world where everyone retreats to their corners, but then, you know, it's a coin flip, 50/50.

Lanterns’ fate hasn’t been officially confirmed, as of this writing. Yet, based on Mundy’s sentiments, it’s hard not to get the feeling that the writing may be on the wall. As someone who enjoyed the show – which also counts Damon Lindelof and Tom King as EPs – I was eager to see another set of episodes. Prior to the Gunn news breaking, it had already been reported that a writers’ room was being assembled for Season 2, but Safran also said the season would only happen if there was a story worthy of being told.

Upcoming DC TV Shows: Full List Of Series In 2026 And Beyond, Including The People Vs. Gorilla Grodd

Mundy and co.’s show predominantly centers on John Stewart (Aaron Pierre), a young man hoping to join the Green Lantern Corps. At the same time, Stewart receives “training” from prickly veteran Hal Jordan (Kyle Chandler), who fears being replaced by the young man. Ahead of the show’s debut, Gunn and the creative team described it as a grounded adventure in the vein of True Detective. Some fans were displeased, however, not just with the lack of green in early footage but with the eventual revelation of Jordan’s death (which Gunn defended).

It was always intended for John Stewart to become a big part of the DCU moving forward, as the newly minted Green Lantern is returning in Man of Tomorrow, which will be released in 2027. Beyond that, however, plans for the continuity as a whole remain uncertain due to Gunn and Safran being ushered out and Jeremy Latcham entering as CEO by Dec. 1. Based on some of the fan reactions to Gunn’s exit, they’re not confident Lanterns will be back for another season.

Needless to say, I’m not sure Chris Mundy’s remarks will necessarily make fans feel hopeful about the future of the show. Of course, the Green Lantern-centric series is just one piece of a larger puzzle regarding the future of DC Studios. I’ll be eager to see what Latcham and the head honchos at Skydance have to say about their plans and whether they include Pierre’s version of Stewart.

Lanterns is now available to stream in its entirety with an HBO Max subscription. Fans can also head over to that streamer to check out more DC shows, those connected to the DCU and those that are not.
