Bradford City 4 York City 0: Clean EFL Trophy sweep for Bantams who show ruthless side again

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

BD8 was packed to the rafters just 10 days earlier for Bradford City’s juicy league meeting with Huddersfield Town.

On this occasion, in front of an understandably sparse crowd, the atmosphere was low octane in comparison.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

For those that ventured out, including a decent-sized following of almost 700 supporters from the walled city - with York making their first visit to this part of West Yorkshire since October 2012 - it proved worth their while, even if there was little riding on this final Northern Group C game.

Bradford were through, most likely as group winners unless something silly happened. Which it definitely didn’t.

A first-half opener from returning captain Antoni Sarcevic - whose three goals this term have all been on home soil - put the hosts on their way to a clean sweep of group victories.

A third cup strike of the campaign for Stephen Humphrys rubber-stamped the win before another striker with a penchant for goals in knock-out football this season in Will Swan provided gloss.

Swan’s 70th-minute strike, nine minutes after Humphrys’ goal, took his season’s goal tally to seven, with just one in the league.

Substitute Nick Powell joined in the goalscoring action before the end after his midweek brace in the cup against Marine. He is now one shy of double figures.

It was a rewarding night for Michael Duff’s side, whose next two fixtures arrive against White Rose visitors in the shape of Doncaster Rovers and Barnsley. This was a decent warm-up.

With the pressure maybe off a touch, both sets of players among much-changed line-ups, showed good things for the first hour, more especially those in claret and amber.

York’s wait for a first away win on their league return goes on, as does their run without a victory in Bradford - the Minstermen last won here in March 1980.

For 60 minutes, they gave a good account of themselves before the Bantams showed the ruthless edge which mauled Marine. The scoreline was cruel on them on the night.

City made ten changes from their handsome cup slaying of Marine, with Kieran Morgan being the only player who lined up against the Merseysiders.

It was a notable night for Iraq under-23 goalkeeper Zac Hadi, handed a Bantams debut, while Harry Ibbitson started for the first time at Valley Parade.

Fresh from his one-match suspension, Sarcevic got a run-out for the hosts, inactive after this fixture until November 21.

Unlike the Bantams, York were mindful of forthcoming weekend league business at Rochdale. Scott Lindsey not only changed his entire XI from the weekend cup victory over Northampton Town, but gave them the complete night off with none on the bench either.

The Minstermen gave a debut to youngster Lewis Beadle, with keeper George Sykes-Kenworthy getting the nod in goal at the club where he started out.

Bradford led at the break, but both sides contributed to a very watchable contest, more especially given that it was a ‘dead rubber’.

The Bantams and their visitors both hit the woodwork. York played some neat passing football at times, but it was the hosts who drew first blood with an elegant 32nd-minute opener.

The hosts broke with intent and Swan’s perceptive left-wing cross found Humphreys, who spotted Sarcevic in space and found him with a lovely weighted pass and the home captain finished clinically, low past Sykes-Kenworthy - as befitting a goalscoring midfielder of his ilk. It was a quality move and finish.

Humphreys and Sarcevic had some fun in the final period of the half and had further chances, with York starting to look a bit wary. They had brought offerings to the table before that at the other end.

Hadi was forced into significant work to make a strong double save to deny Jovan Malcolm and Joe Grey before producing a textbook parry - and positioning - to keep out Dipo Akinyemi’s goalbound header.

Earlier, York knocked on wood from close range, courtesy of Ethan Shiver. As did Bradford earlier, with Kieran Morgan displaying sublime technique to warm up a nippy November night. His exquisite shot with the outside of his foot clipped the post.

Half-time came at a more convenient juncture for York than the hosts. But it was Bradford who carried on from where they left off ahead of the interval, with Morgan - having a handy game - finding Swan, whose rasping shot stung the palms of Sykes-Kenworthy.

The York custodian was then called into action again to beat out Humphrys’ angled drive, with Sarcevic’s follow-up header hitting the underside of the bar.

In keeping with an entertaining evening, York should have levelled moments later.

Malcolm broke away and went for glory instead of slotting in Akinyemi. Hadi, in keeping with his good night, made himself big and made the point-blank save.

It’s importance was soon magnified when Humphreys’ header doubled the hosts lead just after the hour mark following Reece Welsh’s right-wing centre.

It was Humphrys' last involvement of the night.

Swan tucked home a third following substitute Powell’s cross after the Minstermen were opened up.

City gave debuts off the bench in the final quarter to two youngsters in Leeroy Gomes, Gabe Holt and Oliver Hewitt.

Powell added an effortless late fourth after clever play by the impressive Morgan. A rewarding night for Bradford.

Bradford City: Hadi; Welch, Matthews, Pratt (Hewitt 85); Ibbitson, Cartwright, Morgan, O'Keeffe (Holt 77); Sarcevic (Lunn 62), Swan (Gomes 77); Humphrys (Powell 62). Substitutes unused: Doyle, Gillesphey.

York City: Sykes-Kenworthy; J Hunt, Beadle, Medley (Belleh 62); King, Maguire, Stiver, Grey (Farrar 62), Newby; Malcolm, Akinyemi. Substitutes unused: White, Swansbury, Ozisik, Penrose, Appleyard.

Across Protocol warns users to avoid app amid reports of drained wallets

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The protocol is investigating a potential security incident affecting its domain, while several users have reported losing funds after interacting with the platform.

Crosschain bridging protocol Across, which allows users to transfer digital assets between Ethereum and other blockchain networks, warned users Tuesday not to access its app or connect their wallets while it investigates a potential security incident.

In a Tuesday X post, Across advised users against signing transactions or approvals until further notice, without specifying the nature of the incident or whether its website had been compromised.

On X, one user claimed to have lost $9,000 in USDC, while another reported losing 7,053 USDT0 across seven transactions on the Plasma blockchain.

A third user said their wallet was drained after using the bridge several times, alleging that the website had been compromised. Across asked the users for transaction details to investigate their reports.

Cointelegraph has not independently verified the reports. Across Protocol did not immediately respond to Cointelegraph’s request for more details.

Across Protocol responds to user reporting $9,000 USDC loss. Source: X user bilm01

In a separate security incident, NEAR AI disclosed Monday that attackers exploited a vulnerability in its AI infrastructure, exposing account details belonging to approximately 100 users and data from 18 AI agents.

According to a Nov. 9 incident report, attackers exploited a remote code execution vulnerability in SGLang, an open-source AI inference engine, to gain access to an internal server and other infrastructure. The compromised data included user names, email addresses, agent chat histories, settings and logs, while two customers had API keys exposed.

NEAR AI said attackers may also have observed requests processed on the compromised server, including those running in confidential mode. The company said it had patched the vulnerability, revoked exposed credentials and restored access to its cloud services.

The 6 Best Smart Cameras for Building Out Your Home Security

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

You can’t buy total peace of mind, but you can get pretty close.

The open doors era of American life is well and truly behind us. Instead, we have apps on our phones that notify us when a package gets delivered (or stolen) or when your kid gets home. You can have these things set up to be as jumpy as you are, detecting every little unknown motion in the driveway. Sorry, just a chipmunk.

In reality, nothing will give you total peace, but you can come pretty damn close with the help of a smart security camera. It doesn’t even take a full ADP system anymore. All you need is a one-time camera purchase, maybe a cheap subscription, and a phone—which you already have. At the very least, it's a whole lot better than leaving your front light on to deter intrusions while you're away for the week.

If you're in the market for a new security camera or video doorbell, you've come to the right place. Any of these options will keep you, your loved ones, and (most importantly) your treasured, and very expensive, belongings safe. From doorbell cams to floodlight cams to wild ones that you stick on your window, these are the six best (and smartest) home security cameras out there.

When I'm setting up an outdoor security camera, there are only three things I care about: Durability, battery life, and storage. Everything else—like smart recognition, video quality, and notifications—doesn’t matter as much. Give me 1080p resolution, an app that crashes all the time, and no advanced AI, and honestly, I really won’t mind. Thankfully, this camera is a bit better than that with an app that, for the record, does not crash all the time.

Arlo's Pro wireless camera is great in all three areas. First, it's properly weather resistant. It can handle storms, heat, and cold without any dips in performance. Second, without an Arlo Smart subscription ($3 a month for unlimited video storage) you can store one-week's worth of video on the cloud, which gives you more than enough time to download videos of particular events of interest. I wish there was some free local storage (even a couple gigs is enough to cycle recent footage), but at least the fee is reasonable.

It’s solid on the battery front too, with rechargeable batteries that only need to be swapped out about once every month. If you want to pay $199, you can get the version of the camera with an extra large battery, just note it is a bit bulkier.

Budget options don't get better than the Wyze cam. It plays nice with Google Assistant and Alexa, and the newest version boasts improved image quality and better night vision. This 2.5K resolution camera technically has a higher resolution than the Arlo Pro, which is nearly three times the price. What you’re paying for there is a wireless connection. This camera is entirely wired, needing to be plugged in to operate. As a result, the Wyze cam continues to be an excellent option for indoor security, but not one I’d recommend for outdoor usage.

You can pay Wyze for cloud storage, but it’s not the only option this camera gives. I love that Wyze allows users to bring their own MicroSD cards and use them for local storage. Chances are you have one somewhere, and these days ones with massive amounts of storage come relatively cheap. That truly makes this the budget smart camera option for indoor security.

If you want a nice-looking high-performance video doorbell, the new Nest is pretty much the full package. It always sends a crisp pitch right to your phone, and the camera itself has a wide enough field of view that you never feel like you’re missing something just out of frame. And if wired isn’t your speed, you can also grab a battery-powered version.

While I always appreciate a free cloud storage option, Google’s is pathetic. I can’t rightfully say it’s bad on purpose, but after a week of only being able to save 10 second clips you’ll be begging to give your $10 a month for Google Home Premium. This also unlocks advanced AI face detection features that make the base experience feel that much worse.

For apartments, townhomes, or anyone who already has a peephole, this is the perfect doorbell camera. Just pop out your current peephole and slide this one in. It’s really simple as that, no bullshit.

The only issue, as with any wireless camera, is battery life. Since this one is guaranteed to get a lot of traffic in front of it, battery changes are going to be pretty frequent, about every three weeks. That said, it's not a huge deal. The camera comes with two rechargeable battery packs, so you just have to make sure the spare is always charged and ready to go. At some point in the three week period, you’re bound to find the time.

The unfortunate downside of going with Ring is the subscription fee. There’s no free cloud storage so you’re going to be writing (another) annual check to Amazon if you want to use this one.

If you need a camera and floodlight combo, you have tons of options from every brand that makes these things. Any worthwhile one is going to be wired, that’s just how it is. Most of those aren’t bad, but Ring’s is especially good. It’s a wide-angle 2K video, with a 6X zoom, more than enough to get important details when you need them. The lights themselves are bright and sensitive (although not overly sensitive because that’s straight up annoying). The rest of it works as you’d expect, with all the controls and custom features the Ring app offers at your disposal.

The storage is both the best and worst thing about this camera. While I’ve complained about the lack of free storage on Ring devices already in this article, your $5 a month does go a long way. For some people that will be better than having to deal with local storage solutions. Still, I wish Ring offered the option.

So you want something you can move around whenever—maybe from the nursery to the backyard and back three times a week? For you, Eufy makes an adaptable window camera that mounts to your window using suction. Once you have it secured, you can use the knob to easily adjust the rotation. And, when it’s time to move it all you do is remove the Velcro mount and place it on the desired window on the other side of the house.

For something like a floodlight camera, I don’t find the wired setup to be a huge imposition for many people. With something like this window camera, being tethered via USB cord limits where you can place it. Depending on your home, this may or may not end up being an issue you need to account for.

What Do We Look for in Home Security Cameras?

Really, it boils down to a few things. Most importantly, in my mind, are durability, power, and video storage, but things like video quality, smart home compatibility, and motion detection also play a role.

Durability - How weather resistant is the camera? Here, all the cameras shown are IP65 or higher, meaning they're just about waterproof. I wouldn't go for any less.

Power - I'd always recommend going wired here. Wireless, especially for high-up security cameras, can be a bit of a pain to keep charged. Though, a lot of people prefer the ease of setup for wireless.

Video storage - Want unlimited cloud storage? It'll cost you. However, most cameras have some amount of complimentary storage. I wouldn't recommend anything under a week's worth of digital storage. And even then, I'd prefer a local option, as it's cheaper and easier to deal with.

Video quality - 1080p on a doorbell is fine. 2K on a security camera gets the job done. 2.5k or 3K video is where you want to be. 4k video is nice to have, but honestly it’s a bit overkill.

Motion detection - Most cameras are just OK at this. Brands claim their cameras are smart and can tell the difference between you and the UPS guy, but often that's a lie. At this price point, you just want something that can tell the difference between human and car. After that, it just needs to reliably detect motion and capture it.

Taika Waititi’s Sci-Fi Disaster Officially Removed From Cinemas After Earning Just $15 Per Theater

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

He has covered everything from Marvel to the Oscars, and Marvel at the Oscars. He also writes obsessively about the box office, charting the many hits and misses that are released weekly, and how their commercial performance shapes public perception. In his time at Collider, he has also helped drive diversity by writing stories about the multiple Indian film industries, with a goal of introducing audiences to a whole new world of cinema.

Director Taika Waititi was the toast of the town after resurrecting the stagnant Thor series with his trademark take on the po-faced superhero in Thor: Ragnarok. The movie is still considered among the finest achievements in Marvel Cinematic Universe history. It afforded him significant creative freedom not just on its immediate follow-up, Thor: Love and Thunder, but also on his prestige play, Jojo Rabbit. Waititi ended up winning a Best Adapted Screenplay Oscar for Jojo Rabbit, but his career curiously took a hit almost immediately afterward with two major critical and commercial underperformers.

Besides Thor: Love and Thunder, which fell short of its predecessor's box-office haul and is generally considered rather self-indulgent, Waititi directed two would-be awards contenders in the same vein as Jojo Rabbit. While that movie ended up grossing more than $90 million worldwide against a reported budget of $14 million, Waititi's two follow-ups suffered troubled productions and tanked commercially. First out of the gate was the sports drama Next Goal Wins, which languished on the shelf for over a year before grossing $18 million worldwide against a reported budget of $14 million.

Waititi's latest film, on the other hand, has delivered such a poor performance at the box office that its distributor, Sony, stopped reporting its grosses after two weeks. The movie in question is based on a sci-fi novel by the Nobel Prize-winning Kazuo Ishiguro. It features the six-time Oscar nominee Amy Adams and the ingénue Jenna Ortega, the star of Netflix's most-watched original series, Wednesday. Despite the talent involved, the film opened to poor reviews, which reportedly prompted Sony to cancel its wide release and global rollout. The movie eventually debuted in a little under 900 theaters on October 23.

We're talking, of course, about Klara and the Sun, which made $701,000 in its opening weekend and $157,000 in its second. By November 5, Sony stopped reporting the film's daily grosses. The movie's total global haul stands at less than $3 million against a budget that has been reported to be as high as $44 million. Its final daily haul was reported to be just over $13,000, which represents a per-theater average of just $15.

Klara and the Sun received poor reviews and is now sitting at a 26% score on the aggregator website Rotten Tomatoes. The site's consensus reads, "Flattening the nuanced ideas of its acclaimed source material with a tonally broad approach, Klara and the Sun aims to be endearing and inspiring but winds up cloying and twee." In a rare instance of a filmmaker publicly lashing out at critics, Waititi posted a note on Instagram slamming those who've disliked his movie.

You'll have to wait for Klara and the Sun to hit the PVOD market to check it out. Stay tuned to Collider for more updates.

Mark Zuckerberg Meta AI BTC Price Prediction for 2030

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

As of mid-November 2026, Bitcoin trades near $74,000–$75,000 after a period of consolidation. However, a Meta AI BTC price prediction from Mark Zuckerberg’s bot claims that if the most powerful long-term catalysts fully materialize over the next four years, the path from here to 2030 could deliver one of the most dramatic wealth transfers in modern financial history.

In this highly optimistic scenario, several factors align decisively in Bitcoin’s favor. First, the adoption by nation-states and sovereign wealth funds accelerates well beyond current expectations.

Multiple countries formally add Bitcoin to their strategic reserves, treating it as a neutral, censorship-resistant asset alongside or even ahead of gold. Large public pension funds and major endowments follow suit by allocating significant percentages of their portfolios to Bitcoin.

Spot Bitcoin ETFs and new institutional products see sustained, multi-year inflows, measured in the hundreds of billions, steadily absorbing available supply.

At the same time, the 2028 halving further reinforces the existing scarcity narrative. With daily new issuance sharply declining and long-term holders continuing to withdraw coins from liquid markets, the available supply tightens dramatically just as demand from both institutions and retail investors intensifies.

Macro conditions also amplify this trend: elevated global debt levels, persistent fiscal dominance, and periodic currency instability drive a widespread flight into hard assets. Bitcoin, already recognized as “digital gold,” becomes the preferred asymmetric store of value for a new generation of capital allocators.

Bitcoin’s brand recognition, security, and liquidity place it in a league of its own. Competing digital assets struggle to match its monetary premium, while regulatory clarity in major jurisdictions removes remaining barriers for conservative capital.

Corporate treasury adoption expands significantly, and Bitcoin increasingly appears on balance sheets as a strategic reserve asset rather than a speculative holding.

Under these conditions, historical percentage gains from earlier cycles, while unlikely to repeat exactly, still support extraordinary absolute upside given the much larger base of capital now able to participate.

Market capitalization in the multi-trillion to tens-of-trillions range becomes plausible if Bitcoin captures a substantial share of the global store-of-value market.

While the Meta AI BTC price prediction for 2030 is bullish, the market is currently experiencing severe turbulence. Bitcoin Hyper (HYPER) is a safe-haven, early-stage play focused on another major opportunity: bringing high-speed financial applications to the Bitcoin ecosystem.

As the largest cryptocurrency by market capitalization, Bitcoin attracts a diverse range of holders and investors. Bitcoin Hyper aims to leverage this liquidity through a Layer 2 network designed for faster transactions and to support decentralized finance (DeFi) functionalities.

The platform uses the Solana Virtual Machine (SVM), bringing Solana-style execution capabilities to the Bitcoin-focused ecosystem.

Bitcoin Hyper’s goal is clear: to combine Bitcoin’s significant liquidity and established investor base with transaction speeds that satisfy the demands of modern decentralized applications. Key features include near-instant transaction finality and low costs, which are central to its market positioning.

HYPER has gained attention through its presale, which has reportedly raised over $33.2M, showing strong early fundraising momentum. It offers a 35% annual percentage yield (APY) in staking rewards, incentivizing early participants. Token holders can stake their HYPER tokens to earn additional rewards rather than leaving their allocations idle.

With the planned launch date of November 26 approaching, the presale is entering a critical phase. Those interested in Bitcoin’s expanding Layer 2 ecosystem are eagerly anticipating Hyper’s market debut before it lists on centralized exchanges.

In this uber-bullish scenario, Bitcoin reaches $900,000 to $1.5M by the end of 2030, with a stretch outcome above $2M if sovereign adoption and monetary disorder intensify sharply.

Such levels would represent a transformative re-rating, reflecting Bitcoin’s evolution from a volatile risk asset into a core pillar of the global monetary system.

While this outcome is far from guaranteed and depends on an unusually favorable alignment of adoption, macro, and regulatory factors, it remains within the most optimistic institutional forecasts.

For those who believe the strongest catalysts will play out, the upside from current levels remains asymmetric and potentially historic.

CoreWeave beats on revenue and lifts guidance, easing AI slowdown concerns

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

CoreWeave reported better-than-expected quarterly revenue and lifted its 2026 guidance on Tuesday, showing that demand for artificial intelligence infrastructure remains strong despite concerns of a market slowdown. The stock advanced in extended trading.

The earnings announcement lands at a particularly volatile moment for CoreWeave and its neocloud peers, as the market sours on the AI trade and as Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the central figure in the global buildout, faces a scandal tied to an affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

CoreWeave's revenue soared 168% from about $1.36 billion a year earlier, according to a statement. The company recorded a net loss of $709 million, or $1.27 per share, compared to a net loss of $110 million a year earlier. Adjusted earnings exclude stock-based compensation.

Executives pushed up their forecast for 2026. They now see $1.275 billion to $1.375 billion in adjusted operating income on $13 billion to $13.3 billion in revenue. Analysts surveyed by Visible Alpha were looking for $1.07 billion in adjusted operating income, and analysts polled by LSEG had expected $12.86 billion in 2026 revenue.

In August, CoreWeave's 2026 guidance included $960 million to $1.15 billion in adjusted operating income and $12.4 billion to $13.2 billion in revenue.

CoreWeave has lost over 40% of its value in the past month, as investors rotate out of companies most closely tied to AI infrastructure. Rising interest rates have pushed lenders out of the market, raising concerns about ongoing access to capital. Some CoreWeave bond yields recently topped 16%.

"The business can execute well and still face a tougher financing environment," Joe Tigay, portfolio manager at Equity Armor Investments, wrote in an email. "Strong demand is encouraging; the cost and availability of capital remain critical."

CoreWeave will get most the capital it needs for 2027 through clients pre-payments and financing from hardware partners, and the company will raise "a limited amount" as well, CEO Mike Intrator told analysts on a conference call.

The company plans to raise new debt tied to a set of contracts in the weeks to come, said Nitin Agrawal, its finance chief.

"At current yields, we do not intend to issue additional parent-level unsecured debt through 2027," he said. "By contrast, the asset-level secured debt market has remained stable, with our loans continuing to trade around par value." In all, he said he projects under $5 billion in parent-level debt financing through 2027.

Fellow neocloud Nebius, whose stock is down by a similar amount, is scheduled to report earnings on Thursday. Nebius shares got a small boost from CoreWeave's results.

Both companies and several other providers of cloud-based graphics processing units are particularly reliant on Nvidia, which provides GPUs as well as necessary capital to the whole ecosystem. Nvidia shares have dropped nearly 10% to start the week as the Huang affair raises pressure on the company in Washington, D.C. Nvidia's board said on Tuesday that it hired Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to investigate Huang and determine whether national security or company secrets were compromised.

CoreWeave investors are also looking toward next week, when key customer Anthropic is scheduled to debut on the Nasdaq. In its updated IPO prospectus on Tuesday, Anthropic indicated that it could be valued at around $1.4 trillion after its share sale. Anthropic has signed deals for the construction of entire data centers that will meet its needs. "We see that we're going to have long-term relationships with the AI labs," said Intrator, a CoreWeave co-founder.

During the quarter, CoreWeave said it would establish its first data centers in Asia and announced the Forge service for running and evaluating AI models.

Management still anticipates 1.85 gigawatts in active power by the end of 2026. CoreWeave is targeting $37 billion to $39 billion in full-year capital expenditures, which would imply 155% growth at the middle of the range. Analysts polled by Visible Alpha were looking for $37.69 billion.

CoreWeave owed almost $47 billion in debt at the end of the third quarter, according to the statement. Like its bigger cloud rivals such as Amazon and Oracle, CoreWeave is rushing to bolster computing capacity for clients. But last week, voters in the U.S., where CoreWeave derives most of its revenue, rejected dozens of initiatives on data center development.

"If data center construction across the sector were to slow, installed compute becomes scarcer, and the knock-on effect is that GPU pricing may stay higher for longer," Intrator said.

He said the company does not expect risks to deliveries in 2027 based on construction at permitted locations.

"We could sell all of the capacity that we have to current buyers that are in the queue. We are choosing not to," Intrator said. We're choosing to be patient. We're choosing to bring the infrastructure to market when it is further along, because we believe that that will result in better economic output for the outstanding infrastructure that we have."

Meanwhile, demand remains robust, with the company now sitting on a $132.5 billion revenue backlog, up from $104 billion as of June.

"Should we choose to focus only on our committed backlog, we would expect capex for 2027 to remain in line with our 2026 capex investment, while revenue and EBITDA grow significantly," Agrawal said.

Early Infections Prime the Brain for Alzheimer’s

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Neuroscientists from Friedrich-Alexander-Universität Erlangen-Nürnberg (FAU), the Max-Planck-Zentrum für Physik und Medizin (MPZPM), and the Technical University of Munich (TUM) have launched an exploratory research project investigating how early-life infections and psychological trauma can trigger neurodegeneration decades later.

Led by Prof. Tomohisa Toda and Prof. Simon Schäfer, the initiative explores whether severe physiological stress forms an enduring “cellular long-term memory” within neurons and microglia. Central to their hypothesis are long-lived RNA molecules, rare non-coding RNAs that persist for years wrapped around genomic DNA, which may become permanently damaged by early inflammation, leaving brain cells primed for hyper-reactivity, chronic neuroinflammation, and late-life Alzheimer’s disease.

Source: Friedrich-Alexander-Universität Erlangen-Nürnberg (FAU) / Technical University of Munich (TUM)

When an individual receives a diagnosis of Alzheimer’s disease in their seventies or eighties, clinicians and researchers frequently find it impossible to identify the originating event. While modifiable midlife risk factors, such as cardiovascular disease, hearing loss, untreated hypercholesterolemia, and physical inactivity, correlate with heightened susceptibility, epidemiology increasingly points toward events occurring decades earlier.

Severe systemic infections, childhood trauma, and prolonged psychological distress early in life are correlated with late-life dementia. Yet, the biological mechanism bridging an acute immune or emotional insult in early adulthood to the progressive death of cerebral tissue half a century later has remained an enduring mystery in neurobiology.

Now, a collaborative research endeavor led by Prof. Tomohisa Toda, Professor of Neural Epigenomics at Friedrich-Alexander-Universität Erlangen-Nürnberg (FAU) and the Max-Planck-Zentrum für Physik und Medizin (MPZPM), alongside Prof. Dr. Simon Schäfer, neuroscientist at the Technical University of Munich (TUM), aims to decode this molecular timeline.

Supported by the Volkswagen Foundation’s high-risk exploratory funding program, the team is investigating whether severe stress imprints a permanent “cellular long-term memory” on the central nervous system’s core cell types.

Under standard physiological paradigms, the central nervous system displays remarkable resilience. When exposed to an acute pathogen or severe neuroendocrine stress, brain cells activate immediate defense protocols:

However, Toda and Schäfer suspect that this perceived return to baseline is incomplete. Instead of fully resetting, key cells may retain a persistent epigenetic and structural imprint.

“After becoming infected with a pathogen or subjected to extreme stress, the activity of various brain cells changes in the short term,” explains Toda. “Normally, the cells return to their normal state. We suspect, however, that stressful events may also leave long-term traces in the cells: A type of ‘cellular long-term memory’ is formed that permanently changes cells’ activity.”

Under this altered functional state, microglia retain a heightened priming threshold, reacting with exaggerated inflammatory bursts to subsequent minor challenges. Simultaneously, neurons become pathologically excitable, experiencing excitotoxic stress even under mild physiological loads. Over decades, this compounded cellular stress erodes neuroprotective reserves, fostering the amyloid-β and tau cascades characteristic of Alzheimer’s disease.

To isolate the physical substrate of this cellular memory, the researchers are focusing on one of the most unexpected discoveries in molecular neurobiology: long-lived RNA molecules.

Textbook molecular biology dictates that messenger and non-coding RNAs are inherently transient molecules, translated and enzymatically degraded within minutes to hours. However, recent mammalian studies have demonstrated that certain specialized long-lived RNA species persist within the nuclei of post-mitotic neurons for years.

These stable molecules physically intertwine with specific chromatin domains, anchoring themselves to genomic DNA to scaffold transcription complexes and regulate gene accessibility across the organism’s lifetime.

The research consortium hypothesizes that acute neuroinflammation and oxidative trauma inflict direct chemical or conformational damage on these long-lived RNAs.

“We suspect that the long-lived molecules can be damaged by inflammation and trauma, and that this alters their function,” explains TUM researcher Simon Schäfer. “As a result, the stress can continue to make itself felt for a long time: Figuratively speaking, neurons and glia cells remember these stressful experiences, and develop persistently altered response patterns. This then leads to more and more damage accumulating in the brain, until this is finally expressed as the typical symptoms of Alzheimer’s dementia.”

To test whether these molecular scars drive pathology without relying exclusively on short-lived animal models, the laboratories are leveraging cutting-edge 3D human brain organoids.

Derived from reprogrammed human pluripotent stem cells, these self-organizing cerebral mini-brains contain diverse neuronal lineages alongside functional astrocytes and microglia. The organoids provide a human-specific platform to simulate systemic bacterial or neurotropic viral insults under controlled conditions. The team can introduce inflammatory challenges, track long-lived RNA integrity, and observe subsequent cell-state trajectories over extended culture periods.

The ultimate objective of the project extends beyond basic etiology toward therapeutic rejuvenation.

“We want to analyze how early stress can have a long-term effect on how glia cells and neurons function,” says Toda. “In the long term, we would like to find out whether this predisposition can be reversed – if so, this may allow us to lower the risk of developing Alzheimer’s.”

Supported by the Volkswagen Foundation’s “NEXT – rethink neurodegeneration!” initiative, the project highlights a shift in dementia research: moving beyond late-stage plaque clearance to target the deep, lifelong epigenetic priming that makes brain tissue vulnerable in the first place.

How can an infection early in life contribute to Alzheimer’s decades later?

The researchers hypothesize that acute infections and psychological trauma induce a “cellular long-term memory.” Instead of fully returning to baseline, immune microglia and neurons remain primed and hyper-reactive, causing low-grade chronic neuroinflammation and heightened vulnerability that compounds over decades into dementia.

What are long-lived RNA molecules, and why are they important?

While most RNAs degrade rapidly within minutes or hours, long-lived RNAs persist inside brain cell nuclei for months or even years. They associate closely with DNA to govern lifelong gene regulation. The researchers believe these molecules become damaged during severe inflammatory or traumatic events, permanently corrupting cellular function.

How will the researchers test this hypothesis?

The teams at FAU and TUM are using human 3D brain organoids, miniature, lab-grown cerebral tissues containing neurons and microglia. By exposing these human-specific models to simulated pathogens and stress hormones, they can directly observe how long-lived RNAs react and test whether cellular priming can be reversed.

Man Utd Hold Discussions Over 'Exceptional' Nagelsmann Instead of Pochettino

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Manchester United have also held internal discussions over the appointment of Julian Nagelsmann amid links with UMSNT boss Mauricio Pochettino. The Red Devils are expected to sack Michael Carrick after a torrid start to the season that has seen United win just one of their first 10 Premier League games.

Carrick did oversee a comeback win over AS Roma in the Champions League and a comeback draw with Aston Villa in the Premier League over the weekend, but those results do not look to be sufficient to have saved the former midfielder.

The sacking decision hasn't been made yet, but with reports gathering pace, attention is already turning to who could be next in at Old Trafford. USMNT boss Pochettino is one of the main candidates, available for $10million due to a release clause in his US Soccer contract.

But that is an expensive bill to pay for a manager, and United have other irons in the fire, namely former Germany boss Nagelsmann, who has previously won a league and cup double with Bayern Munich, while he also impressed with RB Leipzig, though his spell with Germany ended in disappointment.

GIVEMESPORT's Ben Jacobs has posted on X: "Michael Carrick's #MUFC contract contains a break clause, making an exit more affordable than with previous Manchester United managers. Mauricio Pochettino, one of the leading contenders for the role, also has a release clause, which multiple sources believe will not prove prohibitive if #MUFC choose to proceed. "Free agents Eddie Howe and Julian Nagelsmann have also been discussed. £3m due to Newcastle if Howe was to be appointed."

“Julian coached a Bundesliga team in Germany when he was 28, which is a really exceptional story.”

“Being at that age, now he is in the age I was when I started coaching but he is doing it already for five or six years, which is really exceptional. Still young. “I'm afraid to say, but probably will still get better and better over the years, like we all got, but is already really highest quality.”

That's a verdict to take notice of, with Klopp knowing very well what makes a top coach, and the added benefit of Nagelsmann is that he would arrive at Old Trafford for free and save the Red Devils around £7.5million.

Grok Build coding agent leaks oodles of confidential info, some belonging to SpaceXSI

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Researcher finds 35,000 Grok Build apps wide open to anyone who looked for them.

Grok Build, the vibe-coding agent from SpaceXSI, is leaking data—some of it proprietary business information and other sensitive secrets—from thousands of its users, including the very same Elon Musk-owned company that offers the platform, new research shows.

The information is revealed through links allowing Grok Build users to share their apps with others. Grok takes steps to randomize the URLs so only people who know the exact URL can access the data stored there. A design decision by developers of the platform, however, completely torpedoes that objective and makes it possible for anyone to enumerate thousands of live Grok Build projects. Except in rare cases, clicking those links opens the app and reveals all data associated with it.

Research from security firm Claroc shows that the exposed data includes things like internal business plans, ad proposals, medical exam results, galley checklists from a celebrity cruise line, and employee names and ID numbers. Ironically, some of the sensitive data comes from two-dozen apps built by SpaceXSI (the name of the now-merged SpaceX and xAI) itself. A small sampling of the documents revealed in the SpaceXSI apps—Claroc found 24 of them—include an onboarding plan, marked confidential, for a new member of SpaceXSI’s enterprise sales team, a sales territory map of 594 accounts, internal system addresses for sales team tools, and advertising proposals.

“We were shocked,” Claroc said in a Tuesday post. “The company that makes Grok had its own internal material open to anyone holding the link, and we had never been given it. Had the sales team bought a tool for this, it would have sat behind company sign-in, open only to the team. Built with AI, it went live open to anyone with the link.

Claroc notified SpaceXSI of the privacy spill on Sunday. When no one responded, a Claroc official took to X to tag both Musk and Grok Manager Matt Palmer about the threat. At the time this post went live almost all data remained available with one notable exception: the contents from the 24 SpaceXSI apps. All of them were exposing the information on Monday evening. SpaceXSI began removing the links within a few hours of Ars sending an email with questions for this article. At the time this post went live, links for all 24 apps returned an “app not found” error.

Claroc found sensitive data belonging to other Grok Build users, some working for Fortune 500 companies and a defense contractor. Some of the affected organizations and data revealed includes:

Asaf Aprozper, the co-founder and CTO of Claroc, said he’s in the process of notifying companies. After speaking to the CISO of the luxury cruise company, it “pulled in their whole security team as soon as we shared the findings and are already working to contain the Grok exposure.”

Link sharing for Grok Build is intended to streamline the process of developing and deploying apps. A user can input speech or text and have a working app ready for internal testing within minutes. The links are designed to give developers a way to get the app in front of authorized users quickly. The very left of the URLs contain two or more “dice words,” meaning randomly generated phrases such as “failing chassis traders,” although users can modify them. The rightmost portion of the link is grok.me. The URL for an X ads campaign plan was: https://cinder-crane-sand-frost.grok.me.

The scheme is intended to prevent links from being easily guessable. When done correctly, a phrase comprising three dice words has roughly 38 bits of entropy, amounting to billions of possible combinations. The idea—sometimes called capability-based access control—is that possession of the URL serves as authorization to the resources behind it. Google has long used this technique to make URLs easy to share but hard to guess. A URL share on that platform might look like: https://docs.google.com/document/d/1BxiMVs0XRA5nFMdKvBdBZjgmUUqptlbs74OgvE2upms/edit

There’s a fatal flaw in the way SpaceXSI has implemented the mechanism. Instead of being to the right of the domain—as is the case with Google links—the randomness for Grok Build links is positioned to the left of it. That allows the links to be easily enumerated using passive DNS services. These services monitor central DNS lookup servers. When a server lookup requests the IP address of a previously never-before-seen URL, the transaction is captured in a publicly searchable database. Had Grok followed Google’s approach, the links would have been invisible to passive DNS searchers. Muse, the Meta agent that CEO Mark Zuckerberg said was built from the ground up for security, recently suspended a link-sharing feature after it too was found leaking sensitive data.

Aprozper said a passive DNS search returned 43,601 links ending in grok.me. Of them, more than 35,000, or nearly 95 percent, were public, meaning that clicking on the link opened the corresponding app and all data it stored. He said that tests he conducted over the past week showed that, by default, Grok sets links to “Anyone with the link.” Users have the option to change the setting to “invited users” or “only you.”

A representative with Cursor, the AI coding service SpaceXSI recently acquired, said in an email that Grok Build “apps are private by default and nothing goes public unless the creator chooses to share it.” The representative said that competing coding agents from ChatGPT, Lovable, and Bolt also give users the ability to generate shareable links.

Aprozper’s tests contradict the representative’s statement. In all cases, Grok Build required users to change anyone with the link to one of the other two options.

The Cursor statement regarding other AI coding platforms is correct. Aprozper said that app builders, including Lovable, Bolt, and Replit, “also give each app its own web address, so the same DNS records expose them, too. However, Grok is where we found by far the most sensitive company data.” That means that people using these other platforms are also at risk of exposing sensitive data through shared links. Interestingly, AI platforms that follow the correct sharing link practice include Claude’s published artifacts (claude.ai/public/artifacts/), ChatGPT’s shared conversations (chatgpt.com/share/), and Gemini (gemini.google.com/share/). For all three, the secret randomness is in the path, so DNS never sees it.

The representative didn’t respond to follow-up questions, including if SpaceXSI intentionally exposed the proprietary internal information, why the company removed the data after Ars asked about it, and if it planned to notify customers who also exposed similar types of information. Anyone using shareable links with Grok Build should choose the “invite others” option unless they intend to make an app widely available.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Fossil CEO says turnaround is ahead of plan as sales return to growth

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Fossil Group posted quarterly results on Tuesday that could point to early signs of a turnaround for the legacy watchmaker.

The company, which has struggled to compete with new gadgets in the years since its sales and stock price peaked in the 2010s, has been quietly undertaking a comeback strategy under CEO Franco Fogliato. The company's fiscal third-quarter earnings indicate the strategy is beginning to bear fruit.

The company said Tuesday that it returned to profitability in the third quarter, posting operating income of $25.3 million, compared with an operating loss of $21.7 million last year. It also returned to sales growth, with net sales up 0.4% to $271.2 million. The company last reported a year-over-year sales increase in the first fiscal quarter of 2022.

"We're excited about improving dramatically the profitability of the company through a very strong and disciplined management of full-price sales as well as cost management," Fogliato told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

For the quarter ended Oct. 3, Fossil reported net income of $11.7 million, or 19 cents per share, compared with a net loss of $39.9 million, or 76 cents per share, the year prior. The company also said its operating expenses fell 11.1% to $137 million. The company last posted quarterly net income in the third fiscal quarter of 2022.

With the signs of growth, the retailer also raised its full-year outlook, guiding for full-year net sales of between $980 million and $990 million, which would mark a slight decline from just over $1 billion in net sales in the prior year. That compares with a previous expectation of net sales falling between 3% and 5%. Fossil also said it's expecting positive free cash flow by the end of the year.

Fogliato said Fossil had previously not expected sales growth for another year and a half.

The stronger quarter comes after the company saw slumping sales and financial losses over the past few years. It struggled with the rise of smartwatches, mounting debt and the decline in mall foot traffic.

Fossil stock has lost the vast majority of its value since the early-to-mid 2010s, and despite a recent surge, was trading under $8 a share on Tuesday.

To rightsize the business, Fogliato, who joined Fossil in 2024, said he focused on closing stores, moving away from promotions to full-price sales, improving marketing and broadening Fossil's product range. That meant shrinking the business to a sustainable baseline before trying to grow it.

The company saw a 5.9% decrease in direct-to-consumer sales for its third quarter, which Fogliato attributed to the company's "aggressive" downsizing.

"We simplified a lot of the business doing this," Fogliato said, adding that customers responded well when the company moved away from markdowns. "People tell me they are excited about what they are seeing in the marketplace now."

Fossil added that its key growth engine for the quarter was Asia, where net sales grew 15.1%. Fogliato said much of that growth was led by the India market.

At the same time, growing internationally is only part of the watchmaker's strategy as it tries to improve its performance in its home country.

"Priority number one is to grow our home market, and we're doing that," he said. "As retail is settling into a smaller, more profitable base, ready to grow again, we're entering 2027 with plenty of opportunities."

Fogliato said one key to growth was not trying to keep up with the smartwatch trend. While trying to make gains in the smartwatch space initially hampered Fossil's performance, it's now investing in analog watches again.

Smartwatch sales came in under $1 million for the quarter, but sales for traditional watches — which make up around 86% of Fossil's sales — grew 6.4%.

"Fossil is literally having a comeback, a strong comeback, a momentum, and we can see this," Fogliato said. "And it comes not only through the numbers — it comes with the excitement we see in the stores, and we think we're just at the beginning. We have so much work to do."

Seattle mayor lifts pause and will expand police surveillance cameras with new safeguards

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

by Kurt Schlosser on Nov 10, 2026 at 1:10 pmNovember 10, 2026 at 1:28 pm

Seattle will expand its police surveillance camera network to three more neighborhoods, Mayor Katie Wilson said Tuesday, ending a pause she put in place in March over privacy and civil liberties concerns.

The move comes a day after NYU’s Policing Project released an independent audit of the Seattle Police Department’s Real Time Crime Center (RTCC), which Wilson commissioned during the pause. The audit found the city had built “an important foundation” for overseeing the system but that “significant policy gaps remain.” It made 19 recommendations.

Cameras in the Stadium District, installed before this summer’s FIFA World Cup, will be turned on immediately, according to the mayor’s office. Cameras around Garfield High School and in the Capitol Hill nightlife district still need to be bought and installed, which will take months. The City Council approved all three areas in 2025.

Wilson briefly turned on the 22 stadium-area cameras during the World Cup, citing “credible threats” reported by law enforcement, and said they would go off after the tournament. The audit noted that police streamed live feeds throughout the event, despite a department policy against monitoring cameras without an active call or case.

“After reviewing the evidence, the recommendations, and the safeguards available to us, I believe there is an important and responsible role for the RTCC within Seattle’s public-safety system,” Wilson said in a statement.

Wilson said the city will adopt most of the audit’s recommendations. The police department will publish a public dashboard on how the crime center is used and tighten its rules on data retention, permissible uses and requests from outside agencies. A new advisory group will help write policies for privately owned cameras, protests and where future cameras go.

No Flock cameras in Seattle, ever. New protections against potential abuses drawn from an outside audit. A handful of cameras in new neighborhoods. Here's what the RTCC looks like from the inside. pic.twitter.com/QeYUg8ZkW3

The Real Time Crime Center, which launched in 2025, pulls live video from hundreds of cameras into a single command room at police headquarters. They include 303 police-owned cameras at 61 sites, 116 security cameras at police buildings, 96 city traffic cameras, and 23 cameras owned by three local businesses that share their feeds with police.

Analysts watch 911 calls as they come in and pull up nearby cameras to give officers details callers may have missed, such as what a suspect is wearing or which way they ran. They also comb through recorded footage to help detectives investigate past crimes. The system runs on Fusus, software from Axon, the Arizona-based maker of police body cameras and Tasers.

Police are barred from using facial recognition with the system. The software does include an AI tool that searches video for people, vehicles and other objects, but analysts told auditors they use it only as a last resort because it is unreliable. In one case, a search for a man in a gray top and gray pants returned an image of a pigeon.

Whether the system reduces crime remains an open question. The Seattle Police Department has said officers and detectives are three times more likely to make an arrest with help from crime center analysts, and that the center assisted in 17 homicide cases in 2025, helping close 10. But the audit notes that the department counts any analyst action as “support,” such as checking a camera, even if it produced nothing useful.

Broader research on crime centers is thin, the audit found, and studies of surveillance cameras on their own show mixed results. A University of Pennsylvania evaluation of Seattle’s program, commissioned by the city’s inspector general, isn’t expected until late 2027.

Critics have warned that footage could end up with federal immigration agents or out-of-state prosecutors pursuing people who come to Washington for abortions or gender-affirming care. The audit found that state and city laws limit such sharing, but they can’t fully prevent it. Outside agencies could still get footage through public records requests or court orders served on the city or on Axon, which stores some of the police department’s video in its cloud.

Tee Sannon, technology policy program director for the ACLU of Washington, said the group was “deeply disappointed” by the decision. With the audit having “reaffirmed significant gaps in privacy protections and accountability,” expanding the network “is a step in the wrong direction,” Sannon said. She added that the city should invest in proven alternatives such as community violence prevention programs.

“Used together, CCTV and RTCC supercharge law enforcement’s capabilities to monitor, track, and reconstruct people’s movements across the city,” Sannon said. Protecting residents, she said, requires strict, enforceable limits on how the technology is used, how long data is kept and who it’s shared with, along with independent oversight.

Wilson said the expansion and the new safeguards go together. “We can gather evidence to hold those who commit violent acts accountable and protect our neighbors targeted by federal overreach,” she said.

Business groups praised the decision. Jon Scholes, president and CEO of the Downtown Seattle Association, said the city “already has stringent privacy guardrails in place” and that, with police staffing “still lagging,” additional tools “are essential for solving crimes.” The Seattle Metro Chamber and Challenge Seattle, which have pushed the city to turn on the cameras, cited a poll they commissioned this year that found 75% of Seattle voters support using cameras to help active investigations.

“Residents and businesses have sent a clear message: they want our officers to have the tools to keep them safe,” said former Gov. Chris Gregoire, CEO of Challenge Seattle.

City Councilmember Bob Kettle, who chairs the council’s public safety committee, said the committee will take up the audit’s recommendations, along with those from the city’s inspector general and auditor.

The city also plans to lay groundwork for connecting cameras run by Seattle Center tenants and nearby businesses to the crime center, according to the mayor’s office, citing interest from those businesses after the Bite of Seattle shooting in July. The audit flagged private camera integration as a key area to watch: the police department has bought about 100 devices that let businesses stream video to the center but hasn’t decided who will get them.

The advisory group, which will include members of the Community Police Commission, the Community Surveillance Working Group, police and other city staff, will meet over the next few months. Its recommendations will then go through the police department’s policy review process.

Six Feet of Snow Trapped 29 People in the Himalayas. Their Survival Came Down to the Details

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

(Photo: Screenshot via Aquaterra Adventures)

The mountains around Auden’s Col, an 18,010-foot high-altitude pass in India’s Garhwal Himalayas, looked deceptively calm. It was the afternoon of September 25, 2026; the sky was clear, and the 29-member expedition team had reached base camp at approximately 16,400 feet, preparing to cross one of the region’s most challenging passes the next morning.

But within hours, the weather began to change. A fast-moving depression from the Bay of Bengal swept across the Indian subcontinent towards the Himalayas. The Indian government issued a red alert only after the expedition had reached base camp. From the evening of September 25 through September 27, more than six feet of snow fell.

“There was no advance warning, and forecasting services failed to predict the severity of the storm,” Vaibhav Kala, the founder of Aquaterra Adventures, the Indian adventure outfitter that organized the expedition, tells Backpacker. “The team was caught in a very rare, heavy weather event.”

The group included six clients, three guides, and 20 porters. It was led by Jetendra Singh Rana, a mountaineering guide who had crossed the pass nine times over his 22-year guiding career. Aquaterra Adventures helped open this 10-day route to commercial departures nearly two decades ago.

“This is not a casual trek as the route traverses steep slopes, glacier terrain and moraine, to descend onto the heavily crevassed Khatling Glacier,” adds Kala.

Rana stepped out of his tent at midnight on the 25th and saw clouds stacking up over the col that hadn’t been there earlier.

“Something just did not feel right,” he says. At 2 a.m. on September 26, the sky still hadn’t improved, so he made the first critical decision to call off the next morning’s scheduled ascent to Auden’s Col. By 3:30 a.m., snow had started falling.

“No one questioned [Rana’s] decision, and that was the single most important moment during the entire trek that saved our lives,” Preeti and Raj Chauhan, guests from Bangalore, India, say. Had the group left on schedule, they would have been on the face of the col when the snow arrived. By the end of the day, winds had reached around 62 mph, and temperatures had dropped to about -4 degrees Fahrenheit (-20 degrees Celsius).

Informed by two decades of guiding experience, Rana’s choice that morning came from a place of pragmatism: “In the mountains, confidence doesn’t mean ignoring danger. It means understanding the situation, trusting your preparation and making responsible decisions,” he says.

By the afternoon of September 26, the group’s small sleeping tents were no longer safe because of the snow that continued to fall. At around 4 p.m., Rana moved everyone into a The North Face 2-Meter Dome Tent, which served as the group’s dining tent.

Through the night, the wind rose to near-blizzard levels, and the team felt the tent’s metal poles straining. For about two hours, they all took turns pressing on the tent poles from the inside to prevent snow from accumulating.

“We are not sure it helped,” Chauhan says. “But it made us feel useful and not panic.”

Another guest, Vikram Badhwar, describes a quieter fear.

“It is tough to be cooped up in a tent for 48 hours, but no one lost their heads,” he says. “We were joking, laughing, talking and, of course, silently praying for our safe homecoming.”

Outside, porters and staff worked through the night with little equipment, shoveling snow off the tent and cutting a perimeter around the base to prevent the rising wall of snow from crushing it.

The only communication available to the team was through a satellite phone, which Aquaterra requires all its guides to carry. Rana used it to report the snow and relay his decision to cancel the ascent. By September 26, they were having pre-scheduled status-update calls every few hours.

That’s when headquarters concluded that the group could not walk back. Kala called the Uttarakhand tourism secretary, who escalated to the Uttarakhand Disaster Management Authority. The additional district magistrate and divisional forest officer also got involved, along with the Civil Aviation Department and the Indian Army. All six agencies collaborated to requisition nine helicopters to rescue 136 stranded trekkers in the valley, including the Aquaterra team.

On the morning of September 27, during an eight-minute phone call, Kala outlined a plan to airlift the entire team. Rana and his staff were instructed to clear a 10-by-10-meter area for a landing pad. The rescue was scheduled for sunrise the following morning.

On September 28, the weather cleared for a brief four-hour window. The crew dismantled their safety dome and used the flat surface underneath as a landing pad. Because of the continuing storm, Kala guided the pilots over base camp using Google Earth Imagery from his office in Delhi.

The first sortie lifted off about 8:45 a.m. with two passengers. “Departure order was set in advance with the guests so there was no scramble for the aircraft,” says Rana. Aquaterra Adventures was the only group that had given the pilots exact coordinates and a prepared landing zone, keeping them on track. By 11 a.m., all 29 hikers were safe.

The first day’s window evacuated 38 people in total from the valley before the weather closed in again. Thankfully, the remaining stranded hikers were evacuated over the next few days with no fatalities.

According to Kala and Rana, this rescue was one of the most successful mountain operations in Uttarakhand in recent years, thanks to an unprecedented coordination between state governments, regional municipalities, and private agencies.

Kala subsequently submitted a detailed report to the Uttarakhand Government documenting the actions of his team, Rana, and other guides and stakeholders, along with lessons for future expeditions. His key takeaways include focusing on small details like packing a central shelter, shovels, portable power banks, spare satellite-phone charging cables, and ensuring trekkers are always prepared to be rescued.

“Our objective was to cross Auden’s Col, but our responsibility was much bigger than reaching a mountain pass,” Rana says. “The mountains will always be there, and there will always be another opportunity to try again. But the safety of every person on an expedition comes first.”

‘Confession’ Review: Rob Lowe and James Franco in a Ben Shapiro-Scripted, Stalinist Russia-Set Drama That’s Terminally Tedious

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Jennifer Jason Leigh, Nick Nolte, Emile Hirsch and Kodi Smit-McPhee also appear in Václav Marhoul's film about the brutal interrogation of a prisoner in a Stalin-era Russian jail.

Reviewing the new movie scripted by Ben Shapiro, I’m tempted to pan it on ideological grounds. The screenwriter, after all, is the controversial right-wing political commentator who co-founded The Daily Wire and was editor-at-large at Breitbart News for several years. To say that the vast majority of his views are loathsome to me is an understatement.

Fortunately, it’s easy to write about the film directed by Václav Marhoul without being tainted by political bias. Depicting the brutal interrogation of a prisoner in a Stalin-era Russian jail, Confession proves a dull, turgid slog. The film will be given a one-night theatrical release on November 11, the same day it will be made available on The Daily Wire’s streaming service. Related Stories Midterms 2026 Ben Shapiro Says 2026 Midterms Were "A Wipeout for Republicans": "The President Was Kind of on the Ballot" Movies Oops! How Danny Trejo Accidentally Made a Pro-ICE Movie Confession The Bottom Line Torture, onscreen and off. Release date: Wednesday, November 11Cast: Rob Lowe, James Franco, Jennifer Jason Leigh, Kodi Smit-McPhee, Nick Nolte, Richard Brake, Emile Hirsch, Brad Dourif, Dean NorrisDirector: Václav MarhoulScreenwriter: Ben Shapiro 2 hours 12 minutes

Other than its hardly controversial stance that Stalinist Russia was a very, very bad place, there’s nothing inherently political about the film. Except possibly for the casting of James Franco in a major role, which, like the presence of Jonathan Majors in the company’s recent Run Hide Fight: Infidels, seems designed to give a middle finger to the Hollywood studios for shunning them. In this case, Franco has plenty of distinguished company, with the ensemble including Rob Lowe, Jennifer Jason Leigh, Nick Nolte, Emile Hirsch, Brad Dourif, Dean Norris, and Kodi Smit-McPhee. The story behind that casting would likely prove more interesting than the film itself.

Lowe plays the central role of Alexei, a former literature professor who is arrested in 1938 Moscow, brought to Lubyanka Prison and charged with being a counterrevolutionary. In the sort of coincidence beloved by unimaginative screenwriters, his interrogator is Vladimir (Franco), his former prize student. Vladimir presses his prisoner to inform on Father Mikulski (Dourif), a dissident Eastern Orthodox priest who has published an anti-Soviet pamphlet.

True to the participants’ former roles, the encounter becomes less an interrogation than a literary salon in which the names of Dostoevsky, Pushkin and Tolstoy are dropped so often that a drinking game could be devised from it. The endless verbiage (the film runs a numbing 132 minutes) proves stultifying despite the efforts of the two actors, who give it their all but never manage to be remotely convincing in their roles.

The film essentially takes place in two settings: the interrogation room and the prison cell that Alexei shares with three others, making the claustrophobic proceedings feel very stagebound. Nolte plays a Jewish inmate, giving him the opportunity to deliver a harrowing monologue about atrocities he’s witnessed, which might have been powerful if the actor’s diminished gravelly voice didn’t reduce its impact. Hirsch plays a former concert pianist with an unnaturally cheerful attitude. Richard Brake (Barbarian), giving the film’s most effective performance, plays a hardened, cynical prisoner who coaches Alexei on how to survive.

Alexei’s bitter ex-wife (Leigh) visits him in his cell, exhorting him to inform on the priest. “Damn you and your literary references,” she shouts at Alexie when he resists in typically erudite terms, serving as a de facto proxy for viewers. Also showing up is his estranged son (an unrecognizable Smit-McPhee), angry with his father over the way his mother has been treated.

Then it’s back to the interrogation room, where Vladimir gets much rougher with Alexie, brutally beating him to force him to sign a confession. But his exertions don’t prevent him from having the energy to deliver such ham-fisted lines as “Evil and good are luxuries the revolution can no longer afford.”

Director Marhoul, whose controversial 2019 film The Painted Bird prompted numerous walkouts at the Toronto and Venice film festivals, infuses the film with a suitably gritty, intense tone, abetted by cinematographer Peter Menzies Jr.’s (Shooter, Gods of Egypt) strikingly harsh visuals.

Hopelessly stilted in its execution (it’s the kind of prison drama in which the guards and soldiers speak with all the realism of automatons), the terminally tedious Confession seems mainly designed to demonstrate that its screenwriter benefited from a really good education during which he studied all the Russian literary masters.

Niclas Füllkrug reflects on ‘last time with the three stripes’ as Germany’s 70-year Adidas partnership draws to a close

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Germany international Niclas Füllkrug has reflected on the impending conclusion of the German national team’s historic partnership with Adidas, expressing his appreciation for the opportunity to wear the manufacturer’s final national team shirt. Speaking at Tuesday’s DFB press conference in Herzogenaurach, the 33-year-old Werder Bremen striker discussed the personal significance of Germany’s special black jersey and recalled fond memories of the national team’s training camp during the 2024 European Championship.

The German Football Association’s decision to end its relationship with Adidas after more than seven decades remains one of the most significant commercial developments in German football history. The partnership will officially conclude on December 31, 2026, with American sportswear manufacturer Nike taking over as Germany’s equipment supplier at the beginning of 2027.

“I’m really happy to have a chance to wear this special black jersey,” Füllkrug said, referring to the forthcoming end of the German national team’s historic partnership with Adidas. “An era is coming to an end. It’s the last time with the three stripes. I’m really happy to have the opportunity to take this jersey home with me and hang it in my closet because it has a special significance.”

The relationship between Germany and Adidas dates back to the country’s first World Cup triumph in 1954, when West Germany famously defeated Hungary in the Miracle of Bern. Adidas founder Adolf “Adi” Dassler worked closely with the national team, supplying lightweight football boots equipped with interchangeable screw-in studs that proved particularly useful in the wet conditions of the final.

That victory helped establish Adidas as an internationally recognised sporting brand and marked the beginning of an association that would become inseparable from the identity of German football. Over the following decades, Germany celebrated further men’s World Cup triumphs in 1974, 1990, and 2014, as well as European Championship victories in 1972, 1980, and 1996, with Adidas accompanying the national team throughout.

The manufacturer’s distinctive three stripes also became part of the visual identity of the German national teams across generations. The DFB’s announcement in March 2024 that Nike would replace Adidas therefore prompted considerable controversy. Politicians from across Germany’s political spectrum criticised the decision, with then-Economics Minister Robert Habeck describing Adidas and Germany’s national colours as part of the country’s identity.

Bavarian Minister-President Markus Söder and other prominent political figures similarly expressed disappointment that a longstanding German partnership would be dissolved in favour of an American competitor. The DFB nevertheless defended its decision, arguing that Nike had submitted the strongest offer during a competitive tendering process and that the agreement would help secure the federation’s financial future.

The new partnership will run from January 2027 until December 2034, bringing an end to an association spanning more than 70 years.

The historical significance of the partnership is particularly evident in Herzogenaurach, the Franconian town where Adidas was founded and continues to maintain its global headquarters. Germany’s national team used the company’s purpose-built Home Ground facilities during the 2024 European Championship, creating an environment intended to strengthen the connection between players, their families, and the national team.

For Füllkrug, who represented Germany during the home tournament, the memories of that summer remain particularly meaningful.

“It was nice to come back here in the evening during the hosted European Championship [in 2024],” Füllkrug said of Herzogenaurach. “The families were here. The kids were happily running around the ‘home ground’. Even the night after we got eliminated by Spain in the quarterfinals, there was a nice ‘community feel’ in this environment and it was very nice to be here.”

OpenAI acquires Odessia, travel startup created by Sonder founder

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

OpenAI has acquired an AI travel agent called Odessia, according to Odessia’s founder, Francis Davidson. Davidson says in a post announcing the deal that he’s now working for OpenAI, helping the AI lab with the future of travel.

Terms of the deal were not disclosed, but this looks like an acqui-hire. Davidson’s announcement focused largely on how he built his AI travel agent Odessia by directing teams of AI agents. The year-old Odessia raised a $6 million pre-seed round led by Sequoia and launched its AI travel agent around five months ago. Davidson says that it had helped early adopters plan millions of dollars’ worth of travel, but the tool has already been shut down.

An OpenAI spokesperson confirmed the hire to TechCrunch. “We’re thrilled to welcome Francis to OpenAI. Francis is a proven founder who has built multiple businesses to help people travel, and we’re excited he’s chosen to build with us.”

However the AI lab has not announced new travel-specific products or plans. It does, however, count such companies as Bookings.com, Expedia, and KAYAK as customers or partners. Travel is also one of the main areas that people are using personal AI assistants to help with. And, back in July 2025, when OpenAI launched its ChatGPT agent, it mentioned travel as one of its potential uses.

OpenAI’s spokesperson didn’t have more to share on its plans for travel-related products, except to say that travel is one of the areas that chatbot users frequently ask about. “1.2 billion people turn to ChatGPT each week for help with everyday tasks — from understanding their health, figuring out what to make for dinner, building a budget, and even planning a trip. We see a real opportunity to make these experiences even more useful,” the spokesperson said.

Acquiring Odessia is interesting for another reason. Davidson is well known in the travel industry after he founded a travel startup called Sonder as a college student in 2014. It was sort of like Airbnb, but it focused on short-term leasing of upscale apartments in major cities worldwide. The pandemic badly hurt it, and as it climbed out of that rut, it went public via a SPAC but didn’t raise as much money as it needed, Davidson later said in a post announcing his resignation as CEO in June 2025.

When a key partnership with Marriott fell through, the company went into bankruptcy and wound down. The bankruptcy trustee sold Sonder’s name, trademarks, and website domain in 2026 to TravelAI, and that website operates as a curated guide to the kind of travel rental properties that Sonder was once known for. Prior to its downfall, Davidson had built Sonder into a unicorn with $143 million in revenues. While Sonder was not, ultimately, a success story, Davidson is the kind of battle-scarred founder that Silicon Valley often values.

So we’ll have to wait and see what OpenAI has in store now with travel, now that he’s joined the company.
